- ⚙️ **Rule-engine boundary** — `evaluateEvent(env, ctx)` returning Accept or a Refusal that names its rule; pass-through internals that Plan 2 replaces. `packages/rule-engine/`
- ✅ **Event ingest API** — `POST /v1/events` batch ingest: parse at the door → advisory-locked per-entity `seq` and per-workspace `workspace_seq` (workspace lock first, then entity) + `serverTime` → entity↔workspace guard → permission gate (client door only) → rule-engine gate → append + projections in one transaction → index-aligned per-event committed/invalid/refused results. `GET /healthz`. `apps/server/src/routes/events.ts`, `apps/server/src/ingest/`
  - ✅ **Envelope-origin gate (partial permission gate)** — the ingest service has two entry points, and which one a caller uses is the whole design: `ingestClientBatch` is the client door and refuses any event whose envelope `origin` is `'server'` (rule `permission.role`, reason `Only the server itself may record server-origin events.`), while `ingestBatch` is the internal door and is not gated, because the server's own appends are legitimately server-origin. **Envelope-origin is the provenance enum `'web' | 'mobile' | 'desktop' | 'server' | 'mcp'`, NOT the browser `Origin` header — nothing here concerns CSRF.** Closes the forgery that let a client wear the server's provenance to walk past `contest.server-only` and write a permanent, un-deletable `claim.contested` attributing a fabricated claim to any actor in any workspace. Applied inside the ingest transaction (coordination decision 15), so Plan 10's `POST /v1/sync` inherits it by calling the client door. Deliberately partial and it names its own absences: no actor-identity, role, membership or visibility check yet — Task 5 fills those in on top. Landed here in §3 pending Plan 4's own catalogue task, which may relocate this bullet to §6 — Features.md has no §6 yet, and minting one early would break the mirror `test-cases/` keeps with these sections, which is why the case for it is 3.31 in `test-cases/03-foundation-ledger.md` rather than a new bucket. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`, `apps/server/src/ingest/ingest-service.ts`
  - ⚙️ **Error surface** — schema refusals reach the client verbatim, naming the exact rule that fired; an unexpected failure answers one fixed 500 body and never an exception message, so no SQL fragment, constraint or table name leaves through the product's only door. `apps/server/src/app.ts`
- ⚙️ **Append-only ledger** — Postgres `events` table where UPDATE/DELETE/TRUNCATE are refused by trigger, `(entity_kind, entity_id, seq)` is unique, and both clocks are `timestamptz` absolute instants: `client_time` the claimed instant beside `server_time` the received one, so they sort and compare without a cast and one moment stated in two offsets is one moment; `snapshots` table ready to bound future replay. `apps/server/migrations/`
- ⚙️ **Asia/Kolkata everywhere** — every pooled connection, the dev Postgres server, and every test database run with session timezone `Asia/Kolkata`, so an ad-hoc query reads the same clock the product shows. `apps/server/src/db.ts`, `docker-compose.yml`, `apps/server/test/helpers/db.ts`
- ⚙️ **Workspace stream cursor** — `workspace_seq`, unique per `(workspace_id, workspace_seq)` and assigned in the ingest transaction under a per-workspace advisory lock, is the only order a workspace stream may resume by; per-entity `seq` keeps entity history, replay and snapshots. Server-enriched and opaque to clients (coordination §18). `apps/server/src/ledger/event-store.ts`
- ⚙️ **Workspace stream cursor read** — `readStreamSeq(db, workspaceId)` returns the workspace's high-water `events.workspace_seq` (`0` for an empty workspace): the one position bootstrap, `subscribe.afterSeq` and `caught-up` all resume by (coordination §18). Deliberately not the envelope's per-entity `seq`, which restarts at 1 for every new entity and so silently skips their early events when used as a workspace cursor. Safe as a high-water read because ingest assigns the cursor under `lockWorkspace` inside the appending transaction. `apps/server/src/sync/stream-seq.ts`
- ⚙️ **Projection framework** — registry of deterministic `apply`/`reset` read models applied in the ingest transaction; `rebuildProjection` replays the canonical order; reference projection `proj_entity_heads` (newest event per entity, replay-safe). `apps/server/src/projections/`
- ⚙️ **Broadcast hook** — `onEventCommitted(cb)` fires only after commit, never for refused or invalid events; Plan 5 attaches WebSocket fan-out. `apps/server/src/ingest/ingest-service.ts`
- ⚙️ **Projection verifier** — `verify-projections` CLI re-derives every projection into a shadow schema, diffs against live in both directions, names drifted rows, exits nonzero on drift. `apps/server/src/tools/verify-projections.ts`

- ⚙️ **Environment contract & gate** — `.env.example` documents every configuration variable the product reads; each section names the plan that READS its group, and a variable whose plan has not landed is marked as documented ahead of its reader, not live, so an operator is never told to configure something inert without being told so. `pnpm check:env`, a step of the CI `gate` job, fails naming any variable code reads that the file does not document. Its roots are `apps/server/src`, **`apps/web`** and `packages`: `apps/web/src` alone would have missed `SERVER_ORIGIN` in `apps/web/next.config.ts`, and would miss a `NEXT_PUBLIC_` variable baked into the browser bundle from a page under `apps/web/app/`. The checker recognises three shapes rather than the one the plan specified: `process.env.NAME` access, destructuring off the environment, and — because this server hands the whole environment to `loadConfig` and declares its contract as zod schema KEYS — UPPER_SNAKE object keys inside files that mention `process.env`/`ProcessEnv`, which today is the ONLY shape the shipped code uses. A `process.env`-only gate would have reported “0 referenced in code” and passed vacuously forever, so two guards keep this one honest: finding zero variables anywhere is itself a failure, and so is `apps/server/src/config.ts` contributing none. `.env.example`, `scripts/check-env-example.mjs`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
- ⚙️ **Task ledger & drift gate** — `docs/lanes/task-ledger.tsv` carries one row per `### Task N:` heading across the eleven implementation plans (177 today), and `pnpm check:tasks` validates it. The denominator is re-derived from the plan files on every run and is never hand-typed: before this, task-level completion was tracked nowhere — only step counts were — so progress was reconstructed each session from whatever prose was nearest, and both 167 and 177 had been recorded as "the total". Status is hand-asserted and machine-validated, never inferred from commit or PR titles (four incompatible formats appear in the merged set, and this repo has already recorded commit messages as an unreliable completion signal). `done` requires the PR's MERGE commit — not the implementing commit, which a squash-merge leaves unreachable from `main` — to be an ancestor of `origin/main`; `claimed` marks completion asserted only at plan or branch granularity, counted separately and never as complete, so the honest split between verified and inherited progress is visible on the face of the report. The gate fails on a plan task with no row, a row naming a task no plan contains, a duplicate row, an unknown status, a `done` row with no evidence, and evidence not reachable from `origin/main`; it also lists merged PRs no row cites, the direction that catches implementation work landing unrecorded. Its denominator carries a positive control — every numbered plan must yield at least one task, the coordination doc exactly zero — because the instrument that first counted these headings returned BLANK, not 0, for two plans with very long lines, and a tracker whose denominator can silently reach zero reports 0/0 = clean forever. `docs/lanes/task-ledger.tsv`, `scripts/check-task-ledger.mjs`

*(The eleven bullets in this section named **Design tokens**, **Token-discipline scanner**, **Base UI components**, **Client `DataSource` contract & view types**, **Client submit factory**, **Provenance rendering**, **Pre-check state cache**, **Local pre-check**, **Live query hook**, **Derived progress text** and **Board view** — plus the two new package names in **Monorepo workspace** — are the web-client core's landings, pulled forward as unblocking pre-PRs, joined since by every other Plan 6 surface (T7–T15b): the ticket sheet, list and My Work views, the reopen flow, the founding/invite/visibility screens, the shell, and the one-clock renderer. **§8 WAS CONSIDERED AND REFUSED (Plan 6 Task 16, 2026-09-02) — if you are reading a note elsewhere that says "the decision is clear: create §8", that note is stale and precedes this measurement; this paragraph is the correction.** These bullets stay in §3 and do not relocate to §8. Task 16's own plan text called for opening `Features.md` §8 and `test-cases/08-web-client.md` and migrating these bullets there under pre-pinned 8.x numbers; measured instead, by the time Task 16 ran, every one of these features already had 144 live, gate-passing, cross-referenced cases at `test-cases/03-foundation-ledger.md` 3.1–3.144, none of them under any 8.x number the plan's template ever pinned — the template was prose that was never executed, while §3's numbering is the one that actually shipped. Migrating means renumbering 112+ live ids across every test file that already cites them (the README's own rule is never renumber existing ids), for no reader benefit: the artefacts, the tests and the ids all already agree with each other under §3. §8 and `08-web-client.md` are not created. This corrects `test-cases/README.md`'s reservation note and `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-20-00-series-coordination.md`'s numbering table, both amended alongside this note. No `test-cases/` file is minted for §8.)*

*(The three bullets in this section named **Contest event vocabulary**, **Client envelope builder** and **IST instant rendering & clock skew** are Plan 10 Task 1 — the domain half of offline sync, landed first because Plan 10's server and client tasks and Plan 6's surfaces all consume `formatIstInstant` and the contest types. Plan 10's own catalogue task (Task 18) owns `Features.md` §12 and `test-cases/12-*.md` with pre-pinned 12.x case numbers, and may relocate them there; they sit in §3 for now, pending Task 18's OWN measurement — Plan 6 Task 16's 2026-09-02 decision (stay in §3, do not relocate to §8) was made on §8's specific facts, not as a precedent binding Task 18: Task 18 must re-run the same check (how many §12 cases would already be live under a real number by the time it runs) before choosing. No `test-cases/` file was minted for them.)*

*(The two bullets in this section named **The ONE local storage interface** and **Local replica schema** are Plan 10 Task 8 — the `@gisik-nokma/sync-client` scaffold, landed in §3 because it is the storage substrate the rest of Plan 10 is built on and §3 is where the ledger and its persistence are catalogued. Plan 10's own catalogue task (Task 18) owns `Features.md` §12 and `test-cases/12-*.md` with pre-pinned 12.x case numbers, and may relocate them there; they sit in §3 for now, pending Task 18's OWN measurement — Plan 6 Task 16's 2026-09-02 decision (stay in §3, do not relocate to §8) was made on §8's specific facts, not as a precedent binding Task 18: Task 18 must re-run the same check (how many §12 cases would already be live under a real number by the time it runs) before choosing. No `test-cases/` file was minted for them.)*

Plan 11's own catalogue task owns `Features.md` §13 and the pre-pinned 13.x case numbers, and may relocate the bullet there; it sits in §3 for now, pending that task's OWN measurement — Plan 6 Task 16's 2026-09-02 decision (stay in §3, do not relocate to §8) was made on §8's specific facts, not as a precedent binding §13. No `test-cases/` file was minted for it.)*

## 4. Workflow engine & rule vocabulary

- ⚙️ **Workflow vocabulary** — workflow definitions as data: states with open/active/terminal categories, transitions (including from-any), and the closed five-rule vocabulary (restrict-to, require-comment, require-evidence, require-field, require-different-user) as zod schemas; `workflow.defined` payloads carry the full definition plus an optional stateMapping. Also the one forward state-id walk, `foldStateIdForward` — pure, and in domain because the engine, the server's ticket projection and the offline replica must all fold identically. `packages/domain/src/workflow.ts`
- ⚙️ **Structural validation** — `validateWorkflowDef`, pure and I/O-free so the future builder UI shares it: eleven plain-language checks covering workflows that cannot finish, states that cannot be reached or entered, duplicate ids/edges, and rules that can never fire or be satisfied — including `rdu-unenterable-state`, a four-eyes rule watching a state nothing in this version enters. `packages/domain/src/validate-workflow.ts`
- ⚙️ **Advisory tier** — `adviseWorkflowDef`, a separate function returning warnings the builder shows without blocking publish; the rule engine never calls it, because everything `validateWorkflowDef` returns becomes a refusal. Two codes, answering two different questions. `rdu-bypassable` names a four-eyes rule whose guarded move is reachable without ever entering the watched state. `rule-bypassable` names a rule of ANY of the five kinds — restrict-to, require-comment, require-evidence, require-field and require-different-user alike — guarding one edge into a state while another edge enters that same state carrying no rule of that kind; the from-any edge is the archetype, and a plain fast-track edge voids a guard exactly as thoroughly. A covering guard on the other edge silences it, cover being same-kind-present rather than rule-set equivalence — parameters are never compared, which is what lets the owner's sanctioned fix (the anywhere arrow carrying its own guard) actually clear the warning. `packages/domain/src/advise-workflow.ts`
- ⚙️ **Rule engine** — the real `evaluateEvent`: `ticket.transitioned` checked against authoritative state (transition legality, version walks across stateMappings, the parent-terminal-with-open-children invariant that no author can switch off, transition rules in order with first-refusal-wins); `workflow.defined` checked for structure, consecutive versioning, and stranding. `require-different-user` skips transition-log entries belonging to a workflow the ticket has been migrated off and resolves the rest forward through the version chain before comparing, so neither dropping and remapping a state nor a workflow migration can disarm four-eyes. It reads the log in ascending `seq` — the ordering is established at the point of use rather than inherited from whichever projection appended the entries — and it fails CLOSED: an entry it cannot resolve forward refuses the move ("this ticket's history cannot be read against its current workflow") instead of being skipped, because a history that cannot be read is not the same answer as a history with nobody in it. Every refusal names its rule and reasons in plain language. `packages/rule-engine/src/`
- ⚙️ **Contest evaluators** — the rule half of the contest lifecycle, run by the same shared engine on server and client. `claim.contested` is refused `contest.server-only` unless it arrives with `origin: 'server'`: a contest is the system's own record of a refusal, and a client that could forge one could forge its own exoneration. `claim.contested_resolved` is refused `contest.unknown` when it names no recorded contest, `contest.wrong-ticket` when the contest it names sits on a different ticket, and `contest.already-resolved` on a second resolution, because settling a settled contest would rewrite history. There is deliberately NO role gate on resolution — restricting it to the claimant or to an admin would leave contests rotting on tickets whose own collaborators could settle them, and the resolution is itself a recorded, attributed event. The lookup joins `EngineContext` as an OPTIONAL `getContest`, so every context predating contests still typechecks; a context that cannot answer it makes the evaluator THROW rather than refuse, because a wiring bug is not a claim about the world — without that guard the same missing lookup degrades into a `contest.unknown` refusal that reads entirely legitimate. Rule ids are dotted where `REFUSAL_RULES` are bare, deliberately: a rule id is an opaque string compared character for character by fixtures, the server's error surface and both clients. `packages/rule-engine/src/contests.ts`
- ⚙️ **Planted-defect fixture suite** — 84 JSON fixtures, one per refusal path plus accepting twins, run by an in-memory harness and exported so server and client builds prove the same refusals against the same defects. The harness prior-event schemas mirror their domain twins in PRESENCE and deliberately not in VALUE FORMAT: a fixture may use `t1` and `ws_1` because a prior event never passes `parseEvent`'s door, but it may not omit a key the real door requires — a looser prior door stages a world the server could never have produced, and the suite then proves a refusal against history that cannot exist. Each may carry an optional `notes` string — the only comment JSON allows — for a fixture whose shape is load-bearing beyond the outcome it asserts. The harness completes every prior event BEFORE filtering contest events out of the ticket-state fold, and the order is load-bearing rather than stylistic: default event ids are `fx_evt_<seq>` derived from POSITION, so filtering first REMOVES the contests before they are ever given ids — and a two-list variant that keeps them RENUMBERS the survivors instead. Either way a fixture naming `fx_evt_1` addresses nothing, and a double resolution refuses `contest.unknown` where it meant `contest.already-resolved`: still refused, wrong rule. The filter itself is mandatory, not hygiene: the fold throws on any type it does not know. `packages/rule-engine/fixtures/`, `packages/rule-engine/src/fixtures/harness.ts`
- ⚙️ **Workflow projection** — `proj_workflows` keeps every published version queryable (the engine's version walk needs intermediates and their mappings) and backs the server's `getWorkflowDef`; each row records the ledger position it was published at, so a definition can be asked for as of a point in the ledger rather than only as of now; idempotent apply, deterministic rebuild. `apps/server/src/projections/workflows.ts`
- ⚙️ **Type↔kind binding at the door (G1)** — `workflow.defined`'s registration declares `entityKind: 'workflow'`; `parseEvent` refuses any envelope naming that type against a different entity kind (`entity-kind-mismatch`), the same general door every entity-kind-declaring registration gets (coordination doc §11). Layered with the engine's own kind checks by design: fixtures and Plan 10's offline replica reach `evaluateEvent` directly, bypassing `parseEvent`, so the door alone would leave them unguarded. `packages/domain/src/parse-event.ts`, `packages/domain/src/registry.ts`
- ⚙️ **Entity↔workspace binding at ingest (G2)** — an entity belongs to one workspace forever: `commitOne` reads `proj_entity_heads` after both locks and before the engine runs, and refuses `entity-workspace-mismatch` when an envelope's `workspaceId` disagrees with the entity's recorded one. A server-side door, deliberately not a shared-engine rule — a workspace-scoped client replica cannot see that an entity id is already bound elsewhere, so Plan 10's offline replica must mirror this door knowingly rather than find it in the fixtures. No fixture; proven by integration test. `apps/server/src/ingest/ingest-service.ts`
- ⚙️ **Projection registration order, pinned (G3)** — `buildProjectionRegistry()` registers `proj_entity_heads` before `proj_workflows`; registration order is application order inside the ingest transaction (coordination doc §8), so a projection may read a table an earlier one wrote in the same transaction. A DB-free test pins the exact array, so a re-ordering breaks loudly rather than silently before any later projection comes to depend on it. `apps/server/src/projections/index.ts`

*(The **Contest evaluators** bullet above, and the six contest fixtures now counted by **Planted-defect fixture suite** — one per `CONTEST_RULES` refusal path, plus two accepting twins — are Plan 10 Task 2 — the rule half of offline sync's contest lifecycle, landed in §4 because they are rule-engine code and §4 is where the engine and its fixture suite are catalogued. Plan 10's own catalogue task (Task 18) owns `Features.md` §12 and `test-cases/12-*.md` with pre-pinned 12.x case numbers, and may relocate them there; they sit in §4 for now on the same precedent as the §3 bullets above. No `test-cases/` file was minted for them.)*

## 5. Tickets, hierarchy & rollups

*(Plan 3's keystone plus Chain A and Chain B Tasks 9–13 — the ticket type model, the event vocabulary, the engine-context rider, the reducer, the hierarchy and field refusals, all three ticket projections (`proj_ticket_types`, `proj_tickets`, `proj_rollups`), the server `EngineContext` that reads them, and the ticket history route. Task 14 is this catalogue pass.)*

- ⚙️ **Ticket type system** — built-in ladder epic(1)/story(2)/task(3)/sub-task(4) as seed data plus custom reusable types defined by `ticket-type.defined` events: name, icon, rank, workflow binding, and typed custom fields (text, number, date, select, person), Zod-validated. A select field cannot exist without its choices, no custom field may shadow a built-in id, and `DEEPEST_RANK` is a pinned constant rather than a maximum over the defined types — the rank at or below which a type's tickets will require a parent must not shift underfoot when an admin defines a deeper type. `packages/domain/src/ticket-types.ts`
- ⚙️ **Ticket event vocabulary** — eleven registered types, all v1, each bound to the entity kind it is about: `ticket-type.defined`, `ticket.created` (whose `title` must not be blank — `.min(1)` alone admitted `'   '`, which titles nothing while looking like a title everywhere it is displayed, and which the edit door already refused, so the create door was admitting precisely the state the edit door forbids; it REFUSES rather than trims, as the workspace and project names do, and a padded real title is stored verbatim), `ticket.transitioned` (Plan 2's shipped payload, whose optional `cause` marks a reopen forced by arriving live work), `ticket.assigned`, `ticket.collaborator_added/removed`, `ticket.field_set` (built-ins title/description/dueDate/startDate ride it; a due date and a start date are always `{date, note?}` claims, and `value` must be PRESENT — `z.unknown()` alone left the key optional, and the reducer's then-`?? null` folded an omitted value into a clearing claim nobody made; with the key required that coalesce was unreachable and has been dropped, because keeping it would state the defect as the design), `ticket.reparented`, `ticket.workflow_rebound`, `comment.added` (opaque doc body), `attachment.added` (a `kind`-discriminated union: an `upload` carrying the server's sniffed mime/size and its `sha256` of the stored bytes, or a `drive-reference` carrying the pointer plus the name, mimeType and `capturedAt` last seen on a file this product does not hold). Unknown types, malformed payloads and misfiled entity kinds are refused at the door. `packages/domain/src/events/ticket-events.ts`, `packages/domain/src/vocabulary.ts`
- ✅ **Hierarchy sanity refusals on creation** — `checkTicketCreated` forces every precondition creation has *in this plan*, in a deliberate order, each in plain language: the ticket must not already exist, its type and that type's workflow must be defined, a type at or below `DEEPEST_RANK` must name a parent, and a named parent must exist in this workspace, sit in the same project, have a readable type, outrank the child, and not already be finished. The order is load-bearing and tested as its own property — the most fundamental truth names the refusal, and the one remedy that is an *offer* (reopen the finished parent) comes last so it is never offered while something else is still in the way. Two refusals exist because a borrowed sentence was false on the path that reached it: `hierarchy.parent-type-unknown` (the ticket's own type had already resolved) and `hierarchy.unknown-parent` for a parent in another workspace (ticket lookup is global, so a foreign parent resolves — and naming its project would both lie and confirm the id exists). The project a ticket is created *into* is **not** checked here: Plan 4 owns `proj_projects` and that refusal, and the refusal map says so rather than reading complete. `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/hierarchy.ts`, `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/refusals.ts`
- ✅ **Hierarchy sanity refusals on reparenting** — `checkTicketReparented` asks creation's questions of a ticket that already exists, plus the one creation cannot raise: a **loop**. Order: unknown ticket → unknown type → (to project root) the deepest-rank rule → self/cycle → unknown parent (a parent in another workspace is one of these) → cycle by ancestry → cross-project → parent's type unknown → rank → parent-terminal, and a loop is named as a **loop** even when rank or project is also violated. The cycle walk is not dead code under strict rank ordering: `ticket-type.defined` is last-definition-wins, so a workspace can move a type's rank *under* one it already contains, after which a loop-closing reparent is rank-legal and nothing else refuses it. A parent chain that never ends throws rather than refusing — a cycle already standing in the tree is the store's state, not a claim the actor can be told anything true about. `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/hierarchy.ts`
- ✅ **Workflow migration checks** — `checkTicketWorkflowRebound` guards `ticket.workflow_rebound`, the deliberate, attributed act that moves an existing ticket between two unrelated workflows (a ticket otherwise pins the workflow it was created under, so re-pointing a *type* changes only what new tickets get). A rebound to the ticket's **own** workflow is refused, and that is the load-bearing one: the event sets `stateId` directly and evaluates no transition rule, so permitting it inside one workflow would let any actor who can emit it reach any state without satisfying `restrict-to`, `require-comment`, `require-evidence`, `require-field` or `require-different-user`, and without leaving the log entry four-eyes reads. An undefined target workflow and a state the target does not have are refused too — the ledger accepts only what it can later replay — and each names its own subject: `ticket.rebound-unknown-workflow` names the workflow the *payload* asked for, never the ticket type's binding, which on a migration may be perfectly well defined and is beside the point (controller ruling P13). Who may *emit* it is Plan 4's admin gate. `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/workflow-rebind.ts`
- ✅ **Field claim checks** — `checkTicketFieldSet` guards `ticket.field_set`, the one event that carries every field claim a ticket has. A field the ticket's type never declared is refused by name (`field.unknown`); a value that does not fit its declared kind is refused with the kind spelled out (`"Invoice amount" needs a number.`, `"Stage" needs one of: Draft, Sent, Paid.`), a `select` value must be one of its options, and a `person` value is a non-empty user id — whether that user exists belongs to the membership plan, which is the only place with a roster to ask. `value: null` clears any field and stays a claim, keeping the clearer's id and both clocks — except `title`, which a ticket always has (`field.title-required`). The four built-ins are answered BEFORE the ticket's type is resolved, because they belong to no type and refusing a rename with "this ticket's type is not defined" would name the wrong noun. `dueDate` and `startDate` share one branch and one schema rather than two that could drift, and a custom `date` field is held to `IST_DAY_REGEX` — the same one origin of what a day is. `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/fields.ts`
- ✅ **Built-in rank lock, enforced at the server** — `checkTicketTypeDefined` refuses `type.builtin-rank` when a `ticket-type.defined` re-ranks one of the four built-ins. The type builder locked those ranks in the UI while last-definition-wins accepted a re-rank anyway, and a lock the server does not hold is a description of a rule: `DEEPEST_RANK` is 4 *because* the built-in sub-task's rank is, so re-ranking `sub-task` to 3 released every sub-task from the parent requirement while the builder still displayed the lock. Only the rank is locked — a built-in's name, icon, workflow binding and custom fields stay an admin's to change (which is also how built-ins are seeded per workspace), and custom types stay re-rankable in either direction. `packages/rule-engine/src/checks/ticket-type.ts`
- ✅ **Malformed-payload refusals name their field** — every check that parses a payload renders its detail through one `malformedPayloadDetail`, which mirrors `parse-event.ts` and writes `at <path>: <message>` per issue, omitting the clause where the payload failed at its root and there is no path. Before it, each of the seven call sites joined `issue.message` alone and dropped the zod path — so a `ticket.created` with two ULID fields refused a bad `parentId` with "must be a ULID", a rebound with an empty `toStateId` blamed nothing at all, and a field claim with no value produced a sentence with no noun. One helper rather than seven copies, in the module all seven already import, so the next check that parses a payload reaches for it instead of copying the fragment. `packages/rule-engine/src/refusal-rules.ts`
- ⚙️ **The server `EngineContext` — the engine's reads, backed by the projections** — `createEngineContext(client)` serves `getEntityState`, `getChildrenStates`, `getWorkflowDef`, `getContest` and `getTicketTypeDef` over the ingest transaction's own client, so every read answers with the world as of the events committed before the one under judgement. `'ticket'` reads `proj_tickets`, `getChildrenStates` its DIRECT children, and `getTicketTypeDef` reads `proj_ticket_types` — **all three in one change set**, because a context that resolves a parent and then reports it childless refuses nothing while looking like it has checked (ruling P9). `'membership'` reads `proj_memberships` through **`resolveEffectiveRole`** and answers the whole `MembershipState` — ruling P18, wired by the step-10 integration checkpoint because **no task in Plan 4 owned it**, and until then `restrict-to`'s role criterion was answered null for everyone, so **every role-gated edge in the product was refused on the server path, always, an owner included**; it fails closed, so it was a functionality gap and never a way past a gate. It goes through `resolveEffectiveRole` rather than a SELECT of its own because a project-scope row is not on its own evidence of workspace membership (`membership.revoked` deletes the workspace row alone, and the guarantee lives in that function's early return), and it is answered BEFORE the head read, since a membership head row is the §9 hazard's own instrument and gating on it would add a precondition no ledger path can falsify. The entity id carries its own tenancy — `${workspaceId}:${userId}` — so this reader needs no `TenantScope`; the id is decoded fail-closed, by re-encoding the halves and comparing to the input rather than by reasoning about what a ULID may contain. A non-member still answers null and is still refused, the difference being that the sentence is now true of the person rather than true of this file. The four identity-only kinds return the whole state, `{kind, id}`, from a head row. `getEntityState('ticket-type')` stays identity-only FOREVER (ruling P3): a type's definition arrives through `getTicketTypeDef`, and growing a `TicketTypeState` in the domain union is the rejected alternative the plan text still instructs at three lines. It replaces an `as unknown as EntityState` cast that returned a head pointer for every kind, whose refusals blamed a workflow named `undefined`. Coordination doc, Plan 1 consequences §9. `apps/server/src/engine-context.ts`
- ⚙️ **The tenancy a definition lookup is asked inside** — `EngineContext.getWorkflowDef` and `getTicketTypeDef` take a `TenantScope` (`{ workspaceId }`, an object rather than a second ULID-shaped string, so a transposition does not compile) and every check passes `env`. Controller ruling P14: those two are the only context reads that resolve an id the ACTOR supplied — a rebind's `toWorkflowId`, a create's `typeId` — and a `WorkflowDef` and a `TicketTypeDef` carry no workspace for a check to compare afterwards, so the predicate cannot live in the check the way ruling P12's parent guard does. Unscoped, a foreign workflow id resolved through a store keyed `(workflow_id, version)` globally, the target-state check ran against another workspace's state graph, and the rebind was ACCEPTED. `getEntityState` and `getChildrenStates` stay UNSCOPED and that is the same ruling read the other way: their answers carry their own `workspaceId`, so P12's guard is what compares, and scoping the reader would make that guard a branch no input can take — still passing with `workspaceId` dropped from `rowToTicketState`. An actor cannot choose the scope: the permission gate resolves the SESSION user's role in `env.workspaceId` and refuses a non-member before the engine runs, and ingest's entity-to-workspace guard refuses an event about an entity homed elsewhere — but neither stops an ADMIN of their own workspace naming another's workflow id, which is what the predicate on the reader is for. Four implementations move together (`memory-context.ts`, `fixtures/harness.ts`, the server context, and `contextFromCache` in `packages/app-core/src/precheck.ts`, which throws because it holds no ticket-type store at all); the three in-memory ones read the scope nowhere, because each is built from one workspace's events, which is also why P14's test home is the server's. `packages/rule-engine/src/engine.ts`, `apps/server/src/engine-context.ts`
- ⚙️ **`proj_ticket_types` — the type ladder as readable server state** — one row per (workspace, type key) holding that type's newest `ticket-type.defined`, plus `getTicketTypeFromDb(db, workspaceId, typeId)`, the workspace-scoped reader the engine context's `getTicketTypeDef` now reads through (Task 12) — which is what turned that predicate from a precaution into the thing that refuses a type key another workspace defined. Registered at coordination §8 position 3, which is an INSERT above `proj_projects` rather than an append. **"Last definition wins" is resolved on the event's workspace stream position, not on application order**, and that is not a nicety: `foundingSequence` files every definition at a fresh entity ULID and the envelope schema forbids the stable type key (`epic`, `invoice`) from being one, so this table's primary key is written by MANY entity streams — live application sees arrival order while a rebuild or a verify replays `ORDER BY entity_kind, entity_id, seq`, and the plan text's bare last-writer-wins upsert would therefore have left a different row after a rebuild than live holds, drift only `verify-projections` would ever have reported. The row is instead the definition with the greatest `defined_workspace_seq`, a stored property of the event written once under `UNIQUE (workspace_id, workspace_seq)` and identical under both orders. `fields` is jsonb (equality operator, so the verifier's `EXCEPT` diff can compare it) and `rank` is integer (a bigint would read back as a string and fail the schema on the read path); `reset()` is `TRUNCATE` per ruling P4. The type key is deliberately not the entity id — it cannot be. `apps/server/src/projections/ticket-types.ts`, `apps/server/migrations/1787961700000_proj-ticket-types.sql`
- ⚙️ **`proj_tickets` — ticket head state, driven by the shared reducer** — one row per ticket, folded by the SAME `applyTicketEvent` the fixture suite and Plan 10's replica run, plus `getTicketStateFromDb(db, workspaceId, ticketId)` and `getChildrenStatesFromDb(db, workspaceId, parentId)` (DIRECT children only). Registered at coordination §8 position 5 — an INSERT into the middle of the registry, below `proj_projects` and above `proj_memberships`, because it reads `proj_workflows` and `proj_ticket_types` inside the same ingest transaction and Task 11's `proj_rollups` will read what it writes for the same event. **The WORKFLOW definition resolves AS OF the applying event's `workspace_seq`, never as of now**: the verifier rebuilds projection-outer, so `proj_workflows` is already at its newest version before this table replays its first event, and "latest" would throw the moment a replayed transition names a state a later version dropped — and would stamp the wrong `workflow_version` even when nothing was dropped. **The TICKET-TYPE lookup is deliberately NOT as-of**, and the sentence is narrowed rather than left claiming a purity the code does not have: `proj_ticket_types` is last-definition-wins with no history to ask, so replaying a `ticket.created` after a type has been re-pointed resolves the NEW `workflowId` for a ticket the live path bound to the old one. This table is a pure function of the ledger prefix for workflow versioning, and for type binding only until somebody re-binds a type — the plan's Open question 11, **recorded UNASSIGNED** in `docs/plan-3-chain-b-handoff.md` because no plan in the series has an open task on that table's key. **Publishing a workflow version folds every ticket on it forward** through `foldTicketToWorkflowVersion` in the publishing transaction, because a stored `state_category` a new version has redefined is a lie that rollups, boards and the overdue sweep all repeat; that a `workflow` event writes `ticket` rows is legal only because the fold is MONOTONE — the `>=` early return plus the SQL's `workflow_version < target.version` make the write a max-join, insensitive to the order canonical replay hands it. Both loaders are whole-set by-id lookups, lazily parsed, rather than pre-selections (ruling P1): a `ticket.workflow_rebound` asks for the payload's `toWorkflowId`, a workflow the ticket is not on yet. `rowToTicketState` repopulates the two `TicketState` fields no column hands back — `workspaceId` and `kind: 'ticket'` — and dropping either would refuse every parented `ticket.created` in the product with a sentence about tenancy (ruling P12's guard is `parent.workspaceId !== env.workspaceId`, and `undefined !==` anything is true). **Registering it makes a refusal nobody owns reachable, and the projection takes the safe branch by NAME rather than by symptom**: `evaluateEvent` has no case for `ticket.assigned`, either collaborator event, `comment.added` or `attachment.added`, so all five commit against a ticket that was never created, and folding one would throw inside the ingest transaction — a 500 on a submission the product currently accepts, and a table unrebuildable from a ledger the product itself wrote. Those five are skipped when the ticket does not exist; the four types the door DOES guard with `ticket.unknown` keep the reducer's throw, so a genuinely broken ledger still fails loudly. "Skip whenever the ticket is unknown" would have been the two-cases-treated-as-the-permissive-one shape this series keeps catching. `reset()` is `TRUNCATE` per ruling P4. Task 12 wires it to the engine context: `getEntityState('ticket')` resolves a ticket by its OWN workspace, taken off its head row, and `getChildrenStates` reads this table's direct children — both moving with `getTicketTypeDef` in one change set (ruling P9). `apps/server/src/projections/tickets.ts`, `apps/server/migrations/1787961800000_proj-tickets.sql`
- ⚙️ **`proj_rollups` — derived child progress, direct children only, NEVER authored** — one row per parent ticket: `total_children` plus a count per state category, recomputed whole out of `proj_tickets` inside the same ingest transaction. Registered at coordination §8 position 6, directly after `proj_tickets`, and that adjacency is load-bearing in THREE places rather than one — the ingest transaction, the live projection-outer `rebuildProjection`, and `tools/verify-projections.ts`, which replays projections into a shadow schema in registration order, so the ticket table this projection reads under `search_path = verify_shadow, public` is complete only because `proj_tickets` went first. **DIRECT children, never the transitive subtree**: direct-only suffices for the parent-terminal invariant by induction, because the refusal holds at every level, so a terminal child's own subtree was already settled when it went terminal. **Childless parents are ABSENT, not zeroed** — `{0,0,0,0}` would assert a parent nothing derived — and a `total_children > 0` CHECK makes that unstorable rather than merely promised. **`open_children` is NOT the parent-terminal invariant's "open children"**: the invariant reads the NON-TERMINAL children, `total_children - terminal_children`, which is open PLUS active; the two agree only while nothing is active, and the test asserts both off one row where they differ. **"Never authored" is bigger than "nobody INSERTs a number"** — no event carries these counts, nothing here reads a payload for one (the only payload field consulted anywhere is `workflow.defined`'s `id`, and it selects which parents to recompute, never what to write), and a row that has gone stale against its children asserts a distribution nothing derived exactly as an authored one would. That is why the trigger set is enumerated against every event that can move a ticket's `parent_id`, `state_category` or `project_id` — `ticket.created`, `ticket.transitioned`, `ticket.workflow_rebound`, `ticket.reparented` and `workflow.defined` — rather than against the events the plan text listed: **`ticket.workflow_rebound` is absent from the plan's own Task 11 listing**, and a rebind moves a child's category with no transition and no reparent, so omitting it is live-versus-rebuild divergence. **Every write is keyed on a parent whose OWN stream never authors it** — the writers are the children's streams and a workflow's — and that is legal for a different reason than `proj_tickets`' monotone fold: every write is a FULL RECOMPUTE from `proj_tickets`, so its value is a function of that table's content alone and never of the rollup row's previous value or of the order triggers arrived; live and rebuild agree exactly when the trigger set covers every change, which is the enumeration above. A reparent recomputes the WHOLE PROJECT, because `proj_tickets` has already overwritten `parent_id` by the time this runs and the old parent is unrecoverable without a channel between two projections; `hierarchy.cross-project` is what guarantees both parents are in that one project, and is also what makes the recompute's `min(project_id)` exact. A workflow republish recomputes every parent with a child on that workflow — the deliberate superset, since the pre-fold categories are gone by the time this runs — so no progress bar and no parent-terminal refusal repeats a category the new version has redefined. `reset()` is `TRUNCATE` per ruling P4. Nothing reads it yet: Plan 4's `RollupView` (`src/visibility/rollup.ts`) still ships without the open/active/terminal half, and its cases 6.82 and 6.83 remain open. `apps/server/src/projections/rollups.ts`, `apps/server/migrations/1787961900000_proj-rollups.sql`
- ✅ **Ticket history endpoint** — `GET /v1/tickets/:id/history`: the ledger filtered to one ticket, seq-ordered, actor and both clocks on every event, paged (`afterSeq`/`limit`, default 200, clamped to `MAX_LIMIT` 1000, `afterSeq` bounded at `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` so a digit string big enough to overflow `bigint` 400s instead of raising a raw pg error). No summarising — the contract is `{ ticketId, events }`, pinned by the plan and unextended. `canSee` is the ONLY existence guard; the plan's own `SELECT 1 FROM proj_tickets` check was deliberately not written, because `canSee` answers `false` alike for invisible and for absent, so the ticket's own 404 does not become a second, differently-sourced oracle for existence. Both clocks serialize as ISO 8601 `Z`, so a claim made at `+05:30` and the same claim made at `Z` read back identically — they were the same moment (owner decision 2026-08-20). Registered in `buildApp` as `registerTicketHistoryRoute(app, { pool }, requireSession)`, not the plan's Fastify-plugin shape (ruling P7 — there is no `server.ts`). The response carries no `workspaceId`, so this is the one ticket-read surface a client cannot file by workspace without a second lookup — not a security hole, since `canSee` still resolves the role in the ticket's own workspace, but Plan 5's board is the consumer that must know it. **The route's `ORDER BY seq` sits over an index (`events_entity_seq_unique (entity_kind, entity_id, seq)`) that already emits seq order with or without the clause**, at any size a test can afford to build, which makes the naive instrument (store rows out of order, delete the clause) kill nothing; the suite instead disables index/bitmap scans on the test database before the app pool opens, forcing a sequential scan over genuinely out-of-heap-order rows, and asserts the disabled-planner heap order FIRST so a setting that silently failed to apply reddens by name rather than leaving every later assertion pass for the old reason. `apps/server/src/routes/ticket-history.ts`, `apps/server/test/routes/ticket-history.test.ts`, `apps/server/test/routes/ticket-history-ordering.test.ts`
- ⚙️ **Shared envelope builder** — `makeEnvelope`, the test-only envelope factory shipped from the domain package so every plan's tests build valid claims from one set of defaults. `packages/domain/src/testing.ts`
- ⚙️ **End-to-end refusal fixtures for the ticket vocabulary** — fourteen JSON fixtures carry six of this plan's refusals — `hierarchy.rank`, `hierarchy.cycle`, `hierarchy.deepest-rank-parent-required`, `hierarchy.parent-terminal`, `field.unknown` and `field.type-mismatch` — through the real engine against a planted defect, each paired with an accepting twin so the suite cannot pass by refusing everything: the rank violation and its rank-legal twin, the deepest-rank type refused at project root under its own name, a loop closed only because a rank RE-definition made it rank-legal, the unknown field and the kind mismatch, live work arriving under a finished parent by creation and by move, the terminal child that may move under a terminal parent, a whitespace-only title refused at the payload door as `malformed-payload` rather than by the field check, a grandchild pulled up under a NON-root ancestor so the cycle walk runs a second iteration, and Plan 2's parent-terminal invariant proven in this plan's world. Each is mutation-proven: the code path it claims to pin is broken and the fixture goes red at its own expected outcome, for its own named rule. Two gaps are known and named rather than implied: the `hierarchy.rank` and `hierarchy.deepest-rank-parent-required` fixtures both sit on the CREATED path, so the reparent path's twin rules are pinned by unit tests only; and eleven of this plan's refusal ids have no end-to-end fixture at all (the four hierarchy/field ones named in the plan, plus `ticket.unknown`, `ticket.unknown-type`, `ticket.already-exists`, `ticket.workflow-undefined` and the three `ticket.rebound-*`). `packages/rule-engine/fixtures/`
- ⚙️ **Fixture harness folds the ticket-type ladder and reparenting** — `buildInMemoryContext` folds `ticket-type.defined` (last definition wins, which is what makes a rank re-definition and the loop it enables reachable) and `ticket.reparented` (parentId and nothing else), and its `getTicketTypeDef` answers from the folded ladder instead of throwing. Both folds mirror `buildMemoryContext` deliberately: these fixtures do not traverse the shared reducer, so nothing but the mirroring keeps the two folds honest about what a rule sees. `workflow.defined` stays out of the dialect — admitting it would oblige this fold to carry the refresh-on-publish walk too, and a harness that took the event without the walk would split from the memory context at the seam the shared suite exists to weld. `packages/rule-engine/src/fixtures/harness.ts`

## 6. Server core — auth, membership & visibility

*(Plan 4, Tasks 4–13. The identity surface of Tasks 1–3 shipped on `main` before this section existed and stayed uncatalogued until now — Task 12 is the catalogue task that owns its bullets, and its own bullet is the first below. What follows after it is what Tasks 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 and 9 added. Task 6 brought the table that resolves every ticket's audience; Task 7 brought the decision that reads it; Task 8 is the first surface that ASKS it before serving; Task 9 is the first that asks it before COMMITTING. Task 10 is the first that tells a viewer the size of what they CANNOT see; Task 11 is the first that decides what leaves the server for a device it does not control; Task 12 is the first that proves the role, visibility and session properties hold at the real HTTP surface rather than at the gate or projection alone; Task 13 is the first door the product has for making a workspace at all, and the directory that tells a person which ones they are in. What is still absent rather than missing: the ROUTE that carries sync-down and the backfill on newly granted visibility, both Plan 10's; the narrowing of a guest's definition stream to the definitions their own tickets use, which needs `proj_tickets`; `invite.sent`'s owner/admin slice, Task 14's (`POST /v1/workspaces` was Task 13's and has now landed); HTTP-Origin checking on product routes, a decided non-gap rather than an absence (case 6.54) — `SameSite=Lax` already withholds the cookie cross-site, so a forged-origin request meets `requireSession`'s 401, and the residual a non-browser replaying a stolen cookie sets any header it likes; the per-category half of a rollup ("5 of 8 **done**"), which needs `proj_rollups`, Plan 3 Task 11's; and no ticket titles exist to serve, because `proj_tickets` is Plan 3 Task 12's. Named as open rather than closed: nothing anywhere asks who may emit a **workspace-entity** event other than `workspace.created` — a guest is still admitted for `workspace.renamed`, and which roles may rename a workspace is a product decision no plan has taken.)*

- ⚙️ **Identity & sessions** — better-auth on Fastify (`/api/auth/*`, mounted whole): phone OTP, email magic link, Google OAuth (registered, unusable without credentials), and passkeys — no passwords, `emailAndPassword` is disabled outright. Every id better-auth mints, including a user's, is a ULID (`advanced.database.generateId`), because a user's id becomes `actorId` on the events they author and the envelope schema refuses anything else. httpOnly cookie sessions carry the web client; `bearer()` issues a token for the apps and for every later task's route test, because a bearer request sends no cookie and needs no trusted `Origin`. Identity is server-owned infrastructure — `user`, `session`, `account`, `verification`, `passkey` — never events; membership, which IS history, lives in the ledger and is what the rest of this section resolves. `apps/server/src/auth/`
- ✅ **Session hygiene** — sign-out deletes the session row, not just the cookie, so a signed-out bearer token stops opening `requireSession`-guarded routes even though its signature still verifies: the guard asks the database on every request rather than trusting the signature alone, which is the only way a revoked session can be told from a merely-old one. Verifying an OTP or magic link while carrying an attacker-chosen `better-auth.session_token` cookie never adopts that value — sign-in always mints and sets a fresh token, so there is no fixation window. `SameSite=Lax` and `HttpOnly` are pinned as SETTINGS rather than inherited vendor defaults (`defaultCookieAttributes`), and `crossSubDomainCookies` stays off so the session cookie never grows a `Domain` attribute that would hand it to every subdomain of the zone. `apps/server/src/auth/auth.ts`, `apps/server/src/auth/require-session.ts`
- ⚙️ **Membership history becomes effective roles (`proj_memberships`)** — a projection of `membership.granted`/`revoked` and `project.member_added`/`removed`, keyed `(workspace_id, user_id, scope_kind, project_id)` so that **each key has exactly one totally-ordered writer**: the workspace-scope row is written only by the membership entity's stream, each project-scope row only by that project's. That is not a tidiness choice — the plan text's revoke handler deleted project rows too, and because `readLedger` replays in `(entity_kind, entity_id, seq)` order every membership event precedes every roster event, so live application and a rebuild disagreed and `verify-projections` would have reported drift on a table nobody corrupted. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-memberships.ts`
- ⚙️ **Effective role resolution (`resolveEffectiveRole`, `hasProjectAdminRights`)** — the one safe reader of `proj_memberships`: given `(workspaceId, userId)` it answers the workspace role and the projects the user is on. **A null workspace role always yields an empty project set**, which is where the safety the revoke handler used to provide now lives — and it is stronger there, because it also covers the roster row a legitimate rebuild leaves standing. A bare project-scope `SELECT` can return a row for someone no longer in the workspace; this function cannot. **The rule engine now reads memberships through it too** — the server `EngineContext`'s `getEntityState('membership')`, wired by ruling P18, which is what makes `restrict-to`'s role criterion decide anything on the server path; it takes a `Queryable`, so the ingest transaction's own client goes in unchanged and the answer is the world as of the events committed before the one under judgement. `apps/server/src/permissions/effective-role.ts`, `apps/server/src/engine-context.ts`
- ⚙️ **Pre-engine permission gate** — `checkRolePermission` (pure) and `checkPermission` (async, reading roles only through `resolveEffectiveRole`) refuse under-privileged claims **before the rule engine sees them**, in a pinned order: a claim attributed to someone other than the session user, a client-submitted event wearing the server's envelope-origin, a non-member of the workspace, an admin-only event type from below admin, project-roster changes without project-admin rights, and a **guest creating a ticket**. The gate is handed the **ingest transaction's own connection**, never a second one from the pool. Its docblock carries the register of what it does NOT ask — absences stated as questions nobody asks, never as degrees of doneness. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ✅ **Ingest enforces visibility: nobody may act on a ticket they cannot see** — `checkVisibilityPermission` runs inside the same gate, after the role pass, and defers every audience question to `canSee`. A guest acting on a ticket no explicit list names them in is refused `permission.visibility`; so is a member reaching into another project's ticket by direct id — the same probe Task 8 answers with an indistinguishable 404, met at the write door. **A ticket that never existed and one the actor may not see answer identically**, because `canSee` cannot tell them apart and giving them different sentences would put the oracle the 404 discipline removed one layer down. A guest's write ceiling needs no role branch: it falls out of `canSee`, whose guest answer is explicit-only. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ✅ **Who may retarget a ticket's audience, and who may create one** — `visibility.set` demands **project-admin rights on the ticket's own project or ticket creatorship**, and a claim whose `payload.ticketId` disagrees with the entity it is filed against is refused **before anything is looked up**: it does not name the ticket it rides on. `ticket.created` demands membership of the target project with **no admin shortcut** — an admin joins the project first, an attributed event. Both report `permission.role`, and both run in the visibility pass rather than the pure role check, each moved there by a constraint rather than a preference: the project-membership question must follow the project-existence check so the honest reason wins, and the authority question must follow `canSee` or it becomes an existence oracle. Creatorship is read from the **ledger** — `events.actor_id` on the ticket's own `ticket.created` row — because no projection carries it and inventing one would be a competing definition of Plan 3's head state. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ⚙️ **Which projects exist (`proj_projects`)** — a projection of `project.created`, registered at its coordination-§8 position (fourth, before `proj_tickets`) rather than appended, because the ticket precondition reads it. `checkProjectExists` refuses any event **naming** a project the workspace never founded, and covers the roster events as well as `ticket.created` — validating only ticket creation leaves the hole open on the side it was actually reachable from, since `project.member_added` accepts any ULID as a scope, and a membership granted on a typo'd id used to get the ticket committed with a dangling `project_id`, on no board and in no project list. `projectExists` is **scoped by workspace**, so a real project id asked under the wrong workspace is simply not found. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-projects.ts`, `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ⚙️ **Two doors, and which one a caller uses is the design** — `ingestClientBatch` is gated; `ingestBatch` is the internal append path and is not, so the server's own legitimately server-origin appends reach the ledger without knocking on a gate that would refuse them. `ingestBatch` is unreachable from HTTP **by construction**, not by convention: `AppDeps.ingest` is typed to the narrowed `ClientIngest`. **When `POST /v1/sync` is built (Plan 10) it must call `ingestClientBatch`**, or offline-originated events bypass the gate entirely. `apps/server/src/ingest/ingest-service.ts`
- ✅ **A founded workspace arrives usable** — founding appends the default workflow, the four built-in ticket types, and the founder's owner grant, in that order, through the internal door. The founder can therefore act in it the moment it exists — found, create a project, run its roster — rather than facing an empty shell. **It cannot yet hold a TICKET, and the sentence that said so is corrected rather than left standing** (measured by Task 13's route test): `createEngineContext.getTicketTypeDef` answers `null` unconditionally because `proj_ticket_types` does not exist on this branch, so every `ticket.created` in every workspace is refused `ticket.unknown-type` — founded or not, and regardless of the four type definitions the sequence really did append to the ledger. The half this bullet owns is done; the half that makes it observable is Plan 3 Chain B's. *Owner of the remainder: Plan 3 Task 12.* `apps/server/src/workspaces/bootstrap-workspace.ts`, `apps/server/src/engine-context.ts`
- ✅ **Founding cannot be used to seize an existing workspace** — the founding bypass (which must admit `workspace.created` from someone who holds no membership yet, because there is none to hold) is bounded by whether **the workspace** already has a ledger, not by whether the event's entity has a head row. The narrower question is defeated by a founding filed under a fresh entity id against someone else's workspace, which is how a stranger could otherwise have been granted `owner` of a workspace they were never in. **Task 13 closed the other half of the same bound:** that check answers a NON-member, and a member of any role — owner, admin, member or guest — walked straight past it and appended a second, false founding claim to a workspace's own history, which the append-only ledger can never take back. It founded nothing (the grant is stopped one step later, by `workspaceSeq === 1`), but the name shown in the workspace directory is read from exactly those events. The two refusals stay separate sentences because they name different faults: *you are not a member of this workspace* and *this workspace has already been founded*. The INTERNAL door still commits it and still founds nothing — that door is the server's own, and Task 5's bootstrap and Plan 11's seeds use it. `apps/server/src/permissions/check-permission.ts`
- ✅ **Event submission requires a session** — `POST /v1/events` carries `requireSession` and answers a generic `401` before reaching ingest. `apps/server/src/routes/events.ts`
- ⚙️ **Every ticket's resolved audience (`proj_visibility`)** — a projection of `ticket.created`, `ticket.reparented` and `visibility.set`, one row per ticket, carrying both what that ticket itself claimed (`own_mode`/`own_user_ids`) and the audience that actually applies once inherit chains are walked (`resolved_mode`/`resolved_user_ids`/`resolved_from`, never `'inherit'`). An authored mode wins; otherwise the nearest **authored** ancestor's decision is inherited — `resolved_from` names that author, not the immediate parent — and a root that authored nothing falls back to `project`. Re-resolution walks down from whatever changed and **prunes at the first authored descendant**, because nothing above an authored node can change what its subtree sees. The audience is stored as a mode plus an optional list rather than flattened per user, so a membership change never forces re-materialisation. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-visibility.ts`
- ⚙️ **Resolution restores an invariant rather than applying a delta, which is what makes it replay-safe** — every event this projection reads is filed against a **ticket**, so `readLedger`'s canonical `(entity_kind, entity_id, seq)` order is ticket-id order: on a rebuild a parent is routinely created when its own children are already in the table, and a child when its parent is not. The plan text's handler resolved only the new ticket ("a fresh ticket has no children"), which is true of arrival order and false of replay — a grandchild would have been left at the project default while live said otherwise, and `verify-projections` would have reported drift on a table nobody corrupted. So `ticket.created` re-resolves a subtree too, and `visibility.set` is keyed on **`event.entity.id`, never `payload.ticketId`**, so no ticket's stream can write another ticket's row before Task 9 exists to refuse the disagreement. `apps/server/src/projections/proj-visibility.ts`, `apps/server/test/proj-visibility.test.ts`
- ⚙️ **`canSee` — the one visibility decision** — `canSeeResolved(eff, userId, resolved)` is a pure function of the viewer's effective role and the ticket's resolved audience, and it is the whole decision: a **non-member** sees nothing; a **guest** sees only explicit lists that name them (never workspace or project modes, and never through a project attachment — the guest branch does not consult the project set at all); a **member or admin** sees workspace mode always, project mode only from inside the project, explicit mode only when listed; the **owner** sees everything in the workspace. Admins get **no read bypass** — an admin joins a project, an attributed event, and reads afterwards — and the project-admin flag is management, not reading. Nothing here reads a clock, a locale or a random source. `apps/server/src/visibility/can-see.ts`
- ⚙️ **`canSee` reads membership through `resolveEffectiveRole`, and the workspace it asks about is the TICKET's** — never a direct project-scope `SELECT` on `proj_memberships`: a revocation deletes only the workspace-scope row, so a project-scope row **survives** and a bare read returns rows for people no longer in the workspace. `canSee` also takes no `workspaceId` parameter — it resolves the role in the workspace the ticket's own row names, so a role held in one workspace answers nothing about another's ticket. A ticket with no `proj_visibility` row answers `false`, identically to one the viewer may not see. `apps/server/src/visibility/can-see.ts`
- ⚙️ **`listVisibleTickets` — the same table as one SQL predicate** — a viewer's whole slice of a workspace in one round trip rather than N. It is the decision table **said twice**, which is the two-sources-of-truth shape this series keeps catching, so the cost is paid by a test rather than a comment: every viewer class in one fixture is asserted both against its exact expected id set and against `canSee` ticket by ticket, with a non-vacuity assertion so the agreement cannot be agreement about two empty answers. It returns `proj_visibility` rows today — **`proj_tickets` does not exist on this branch** (Plan 3 Task 12 owns it) and no competing definition of it is invented here; the join lands with that table, behind the same WHERE clause and the same tests. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-tickets.ts`
- ✅ **Reads enforce visibility, and an invisible ticket is indistinguishable from one that never existed** — `GET /v1/workspaces/:workspaceId/tickets`, `.../tickets/:ticketId` and `.../tickets/:ticketId/events`, all three behind `requireSession` and all three deferring every audience question to `canSee`. A direct read of a ticket the viewer may not see answers `404 {error:'not_found'}` with the **same status, body and headers** as a read of an id that never existed, so a member probing another project's ids learns nothing; the history door is refused identically, because it is a separate registration that would otherwise have to be refused separately. On the collection the same rule runs the other way: an outsider gets `200 { tickets: [] }`, never a refusal, since an empty/non-empty distinction would be the oracle the 404 discipline removes. The route adds exactly ONE question of its own — is this ticket in the workspace the path names — which is identity, not audience: `canSee` deliberately takes no workspace, so without it a member of two workspaces would read B's ticket through an A-shaped URL. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **The restricted-parent placeholder, and the existence it must NOT invent** — a visible child under a parent it may not see returns `parent: { kind: 'restricted', id }`: existence and id, structurally unable to carry more, so the child never poses as parentless. That is the one deliberate existence leak in the product (spec §6), reachable only through a child the viewer can already see. Its inverse is closed in the same branch: `canSee` answers `false` alike for an invisible ticket and for one with **no row at all**, so collapsing the two would assert the existence of a ticket nothing has evidence for — a `parent_id` naming no ticket returns `parent: null`, the same answer `proj_visibility` gave when it resolved that child as a root. Rows are `proj_visibility`'s and there are **no ticket titles anywhere queryable** on this branch, so the placeholder shipped as existence-versus-the-row rather than existence-versus-title — **and P25 clause 1 inverted that**: the visible parent arm now carries a `title`, so it ships as existence-versus-title, which is the condition this sentence used to record as unmet. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **A served row goes out through an allow-list, because one of `proj_visibility`'s columns is a ticket id** — `resolved_from` names the nearest *authored* ancestor, which is deliberately not the immediate parent, so `SELECT *` would have disclosed the existence of a ticket that is neither visible nor the parent: exactly the leak the placeholder is bounded to avoid. It is cut, along with the explicit audience rosters, in the one mapper both the list and the single read go through; `parent_id` stays, being the same fact as the placeholder itself. The **history endpoint is deliberately not trimmed the same way** — the ancestor ids in a `ticket.created` or `ticket.reparented` payload are provenance, an attributed claim, and spec §6 says provenance is never redacted. Cut derived state; never cut a claim. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **Provenance is never redacted from someone entitled to the state** — ticket history is the ledger, filtered: full envelopes with `actor_id` and both clocks, unchanged even when the actor shares no membership at all with the viewer. Both instants are rendered as absolute ISO 8601 `Z` explicitly rather than left to the serializer, because the session runs in Asia/Kolkata and any other rendering would put an offset label into a field every client parses as a moment. There is deliberately **no second, anonymizing serialization path** — a redacting path that exists is a path something can drift into. `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **Rollup honesty — the true total, with the restriction NAMED** — the single-ticket read carries `rollup: { total, restrictedCount } | null`. `total` counts **every** direct child with no visibility filtering whatever, and the part the viewer cannot reach is disclosed beside it rather than subtracted out of it, because a rollup showing only what a viewer can see understates the work and makes a parent look complete when it is not (spec §6). The asymmetry is the design: **`total` is per-parent and `restrictedCount` is per-viewer**, so two people reading the same parent get the same total and different restrictions. There is no third `visibleCount` field: one shipped and was **removed after measurement** — mutating its counter to `total - restrictedCount` killed nothing in the whole server suite, so the invariant defending it was decoration, and the enumeration is cross-checked against `canSee` child by child instead, which is an independent witness rather than arithmetic. The viewer's role is resolved once per **distinct child workspace** — the workspace is the ticket's, never the caller's — rather than once from the first child, and a child in a foreign workspace is counted in the total rather than quietly dropped. **The per-category half is absent rather than zeroed**: "5 of 8 **done**" needs each child's `state_category`, which lives in `proj_tickets` via `proj_rollups`, and neither table exists on this branch (Plan 3 Tasks 11 and 12) — so `open`, `active` and `terminal` are left off the type entirely, since a `0` would be a lie and a `null` still invites a client to render the phrase, while an absent key makes the compiler demand a decision the day the table lands. A childless ticket answers `null`, not a zeroed family, matching what `proj_rollups` will store. `apps/server/src/visibility/rollup.ts`, `apps/server/src/routes/tickets.ts`
- ✅ **The sync-down visibility filter — what leaves the server for a device it does not control** — `visibleEventsFor(db, userId, workspaceId, afterSeq)` streams a workspace's ledger past a cursor, filtered against visibility **as of now**, and yields `LedgerEvent | PurgeInstruction | ScopeRevokedInstruction`. An event about a ticket the viewer cannot see now is never yielded — **not the payload and not the id**, because an envelope naming a ticket while withholding its body still discloses that the ticket exists, which is the oracle the read door spent a whole task closing. What a SCOPE REVOCATION inside the window took away comes back as a tombstone instead, since an event that merely stops arriving leaves the data on the device forever: losing a project purges that project's now-invisible tickets, and a revoke-and-re-grant purges what the new role no longer reaches. **A purge is a per-BOUNDARY instruction and only a per-boundary one.** There was a second emitter — a per-EVENT walk that ran on every `visibility.set` and `ticket.reparented`, purging that ticket and every inheriting descendant the viewer cannot see NOW — and a controller ruling struck it. A purge names "cannot see NOW", which is true of a ticket taken away and equally true of one never held; the module cannot tell them apart, because `proj_visibility` keeps no prior audience and neither payload carries a before-image, and re-deriving the projection's `resolve()` rule here against a ledger lookback would be a second definition of visibility that can disagree with the table every other door reads. **What makes an id tolerable is RELATIONSHIP, not size.** The read door's restricted-parent disclosure is acceptable because the viewer already HOLDS the child whose parent id it names; the walk had no such axis — its own test seeded a ticket in a project the viewer was never on and watched the walk purge it — so it was the guest disclosure at a smaller radius, and the cardinality defence for it compared a per-emission quantity against a per-boundary one. It could not be gated on the property it needed, so it is removed rather than approximated. What survives the ruling: a **guest**, and anyone the range put out of the workspace, is never handed a ticket id at all — they are answered at scope level or not at all; at a **scope-revocation boundary** a member or admin still standing inside the workspace may be handed an id they never held, which is the existence-leak spec §6 accepted, bounded by the named scope and fired once for a named person. The cost is recorded rather than hidden and it now falls on guests and members alike: someone un-shared from a ticket by a plain `visibility.set` or `ticket.reparented` keeps the stale copy until a full resync, taken in that direction because an id on a device the server does not control is never retrieved while retained data is corrected by the next bootstrap (owner: Plan 10). `afterSeq` is the **workspace** cursor (`workspace_seq`, coordination §18) and never a per-entity `seq` — every entity restarts its own counter at 1, so ordering the stream by it would bury the entire early history of anything born after the cursor. Membership is asked only through `resolveEffectiveRole`, never off `proj_memberships`, whose project-scope rows survive a revocation by design. The walk's **cycle guard** left with it: the parent graph can contain a loop and nothing on this branch refuses one (case 6.58), and a walk that descended `parent_id` turned that loop into unbounded recursion inside the generator serving every replica — a hang on a request path rather than a wrong answer. The scope scans iterate rows and do not recurse, so this module has no cycle hazard left; refusing a cycle-creating reparent at the door is still Plan 3's. Non-ticket traffic splits by role: workspace, project and membership events go to non-guests; workflow and ticket-type definitions go to everyone, guests included, because a guest cannot render their explicitly-shared ticket's status without the workflow that names its states. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`
- ✅ **A device whose owner was put out of the workspace is told, and the plan's own code could not tell it** — the plan text opened with `if (eff.workspaceRole === null) return;` and placed the `membership.revoked` purge branch *inside* the loop below it. Those two lines cannot both run for the same person: a revocation deletes the workspace-scope row, so the revoked user's role **is** null and the generator returned before reaching the branch written to serve them. Every ticket on that replica would have stayed there. The discriminator is the **range, not the role** — a stranger's window contains no `membership.revoked` naming them, and someone revoked before the cursor was already told — so a null role plus a revocation in range yields one `{kind:'scope-revoked'}` and stops. It is necessarily scope-level: itemising would mean asking which tickets are invisible to someone invisible to everything, which returns the **whole workspace**, handed as a count to a person now standing outside it. A guest is answered the same way and for the same reason; a member still inside the workspace's accepted existence-leak gets the itemised purges — an arm that shipped executed by **no** test, and whose fixture had to be an **owner** re-granted as a member, since only the owner bypass's loss makes the itemised list a set of genuine retractions rather than a list of ids the recipient never held. On the degenerate shape — revoked and re-granted at `member` inside one window — the recipient has lost nothing at all, because the revocation deletes only the workspace-scope row and the project rows survive it, so every id that arm emits is one they never held; that is stated in the code rather than papered over, and widening the scope instruction to cover it was declined because it would wipe a replica that lost nothing. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`
- ⚙️ **Which entity kinds may travel is decided by the compiler** — `audienceClassOf` maps every `EntityKind` to one of `ticket · roster · workspace · definition · withheld` through a **bare switch with no `default`**, the idiom `canSeeResolved` established: a kind no `case` names falls out, the function ends without returning, and tsc refuses it with TS2366. Measured rather than asserted — adding a member to `ENTITY_KINDS` raises TS2366 at that function and, on this branch, **nowhere else in the server**, so it is the only place a new kind is forced to declare an audience. Without it a kind added by a later plan would be silently dropped from every replica, or — under a permissive `default` — silently sent to everyone. `invite` is classified `withheld` from **everyone**, owners included: `invite.sent` carries an email address, the owner/admin slice that should carry it is Task 13's, and shipping it to every member meanwhile would disclose the PII that slice exists to protect. `apps/server/src/visibility/visible-events-for.ts`
- ⚙️ **`EffectiveRole` is branded, so its provenance is a constraint rather than a convention** — a module-private `unique symbol` that only `effective-role.ts` can write, closing ruling P22. Every safety property proved of `canSeeResolved` rests on its `EffectiveRole` having come from `resolveEffectiveRole`, which is the only thing that makes a null workspace role imply an empty project set; TypeScript is structural, so before the brand any object of the right shape was accepted and a hand-built or stale one would have carried a project set no early return had emptied. It does not make hand-building impossible — two pure-function test helpers legitimately cast — it makes it a **deliberate, greppable assertion at a named site** instead of an accident. `apps/server/src/permissions/effective-role.ts`
- ⚙️ **Scenario seeds built from real events** — `seedMember`, `seedGuest`, `revokeMember`, `seedProject`, `seedTicket`, `setVisibilityEnv` drive the **real projections** with the **real event payloads** rather than writing the projection tables by hand. That is what makes the revoked-member case honest: the surviving project-scope row has to arrive the way a real revocation leaves it, or a test built on it would pass with the safety property deleted. `seedTicket` also appends its `ticket.created` to the **ledger**, because creatorship is read from there and a projection-only fixture would leave every creator lookup answering null. `apps/server/test/helpers/seed.ts`
- ⚙️ **Canonical simple-Kanban workflow template** — the definition a new workspace is founded on, deep-cloned per use so editing a clone can never reach the template. `packages/domain/src/simple-kanban.ts`
