The LANE is still not passed, and the other nine axes are still not reachable. **A hard ceiling found while wiring it, worth knowing before designing the rest:** the six id-shaped axes are LISTS client-side while `filterSpecSchema` takes exactly ONE value each, so `toServerFilterSpec` THROWS `UnexpressibleFilterError` on any two-value narrowing — *"not done"* (`open` + `active`) cannot be expressed at all, and needs either a plural `stateCategory` or a negation term on the route. The `data-pending` half is a different case and NOT a gap: `toTicketDatesInput` hard-codes `pendingFieldIds: []` because this shell is online-only, so at the moment a list read lands nothing is in transit — Plan 10's replica is where that array stops being empty. *Owner for the filter wiring: whoever mounts a filter bar on the project page.* `packages/app-core/src/filter/`, `packages/app-core/src/claims/`, `apps/web/src/ticket-dates-input.ts`
- ⚙️ **Notification fan-out** — on committed events: `ticket.assigned` → assignee, structured `@mention` nodes in comments/descriptions → the mentioned, `claim.contested` → the original claimant; canSee-gated so a notification never out-leaks its ticket; idempotent per (user, dedupe key). `apps/server/src/notifications/fanout.ts`
- ⚙️ **Attention sweep** — deterministic, idempotent job: dueDate claims entering the warning window and active states outliving the staleness threshold become inbox rows carrying the claim's provenance verbatim; running twice notifies once, including across midnight. Both windows are the workspace's own settings, resolved once per workspace from `getWorkspaceSettings` — the sweep's "approaching" window is `dueSoonDays` and its staleness threshold is `staleDays`, the same two the attention strip reads, so an admin who moves one moves the board and the inbox together and the sweep holds no threshold of its own. `apps/server/src/notifications/sweep.ts`
- ⚙️ **IST day boundaries everywhere a day is decided** — calendar cell assignment, timeline day columns, the today ring, the sweep's warning horizon and staleness window, and the inbox's "since" all resolve a day through the domain's `istDayOf`/`istDayBounds`; day-vs-instant comparisons are half-open `[startUtc, endUtc)`. Rendered clocks use `IST_TIME_ZONE` from the domain, never a literal and never the device. `packages/app-core/src/claims/`, `apps/server/src/notifications/sweep.ts`, `packages/app-core/src/inbox/format.ts`
- ⚙️ **Notification storage + API** — per-user `notifications` rows (server-owned, NOT ledger events, NOT a projection — replay can never re-invent a nag); `GET /v1/me/notifications` (cursor), unread count, mark-read; strict payload schemas with no field for invented urgency. `apps/server/src/notifications/store.ts`, `apps/server/src/routes/notifications.ts`
- ⚙️ **NotificationTransport seam** — at-least-once per (user, dedupeKey); in-app transport ships here, web push (Plan 10) and native push (Plan 11) implement the same interface. `apps/server/src/notifications/transport.ts`
- ✅ **Inbox** — notification list with per-kind icons, deep links, unread badge, mark-read; headlines are the claims' own attributed wording. Its HTTP adapter is `packages/app-core/src/online/notifications.ts` — moved there from `apps/web/src/` on 2026-09-07 to sit with its three siblings, which is what made the consumer round-trippable from a server test at all. `packages/app-core/src/inbox/`, `apps/web/app/inbox/`
- ⚙️ **Saved filters** — named FilterSpecs stored via `/v1/me/preferences`; applying one is a view act, never a data act. **⚙️ and not ✅ deliberately: nothing mounts `SavedFiltersMenu` and no `PreferencesGateway` implementation exists outside the test fake**, which `saved-filters.ts`'s own docblock already concedes — so the store, the schema and the menu are all real and tested, and no user can reach any of them yet. *Owner: whoever mounts the menu and writes the gateway against Plan 5's whole-document `GET`/`PUT /v1/me/preferences`.* `packages/app-core/src/preferences/`
- ⚙️ **Reporting scope guard** — an executable scan across seven client and server source trees (`packages/app-core/src`, `packages/domain/src`, `packages/rule-engine/src`, `packages/ui/src`, `apps/server/src`, `apps/web/src`, `apps/web/app` — the last two added because that is where days are finally drawn) keeps velocity/burndown/forecast vocabulary out of every one of them, and keeps the zone string and the day regex to their single origin in the domain. `packages/app-core/test/reporting-scope-guard.test.ts`

## 13. Deployment & dogfooding

*(Plan 11's own Task 16 opens this section by convention, held until Tasks 1–15 merge. In practice it was
opened early, incrementally, by Tasks 1–15 across five parallel lanes — so what Task 16 did was give it
its FINAL SHAPE, reconciling what was already here rather than writing it fresh. **All sixteen tasks have
now landed**, and the runbook named throughout this section exists at `docs/runbook.md`. What remains
outside it is the live production deploy itself, which is out of scope by the owner's instruction: the
DigitalOcean spec is written and quarantined in `deploy/do/`, and provisioning is verified at deploy time
(`13.21`), not by CI.)*

- ⚙️ **Graceful shutdown on SIGTERM / SIGINT** — `installShutdownHandlers(app, pool)` is installed by
  the one non-test boot path BEFORE `app.listen`, and runs `app.close()` (bounded 5 s) → `pool.end()`
  (bounded 2 s), then lets the event loop drain to exit 0; a whole-process watchdog exits 1 at 8 s if
  something still holds the loop. The bounds are sized under `docker stop`'s 10 s default, the smallest
  grace period this image meets in the wild. **The defect it closes is a PID-1 one, measured against
  the built image, not reasoned about**: `apps/server/Dockerfile`'s entrypoint `exec`s node, so node is
  PID 1, and PID 1 has no default signal dispositions — with no handler installed, SIGTERM was
  **ignored** and every deploy ended in SIGKILL after the grace period (`docker stop -t 3`: 3197 ms
  ignored vs 172 ms handled), severing in-flight requests and leaving the snapshot writer's interval,
  the stream hub's sockets, the notification fan-out's subscription and the pool all unreleased. A
  second signal is logged and dropped (`pool.end()` twice throws). Process-level `unhandledRejection` /
  `uncaughtException` handlers are deliberately NOT installed for the life of the process — they
  suppress Node's own crash — with ONE narrow exception, installed only for the duration of a shutdown
  and removed after it: avvio runs `onClose` hooks synchronously, so a hook that throws
  **synchronously** never rejects `app.close()`'s promise and is not catchable there; measured without
  the exception, that fault skipped `pool.end()` entirely and exited 1 in 12 ms — a graceful stop
  reported to the orchestrator as a crash. `apps/server/src/shutdown.ts`, called from
  `apps/server/src/index.ts`
- ⚙️ **Seed plumbing** — `seedUlid(namespace, key)` mints a deterministic, `ULID_REGEX`-valid id from a SHA-256 digest, so re-running a seed regenerates identical event ids and a re-run can recognise its own prior work rather than duplicating it. (The recognition is a ledger lookup BEFORE submission, not the plan text's `duplicate-event-id` refusal — measured in Task 9: the permission gate and the rule engine both answer before `insertEvent` ever reaches the primary key, so that refusal is unreachable for most seed events at either door.) `plainTextDoc(text)` builds a literal-text ProseMirror doc, one paragraph per line, by delegating each line to the domain's own single-paragraph `plainTextToDoc` rather than duplicating its paragraph-shape logic. `findUserIdByEmail` looks a person up in better-auth's `"user"` table by email — seeds never invent people. `apps/server/src/seed/{seed-ulid,doc-text,users}.ts`
- ⚙️ **`checkErrandTemplate` — the executable pin on the errand-with-waits template, and Ruling P29's tripwire** — verifies Plan 8's `ERRAND_WITH_WAITS_TEMPLATE` can structurally carry the Convenience Corner tracker board before any seed event is built: every tracker state the seed maps to is present and reachable from the initial state in exactly one transition (no fabricated intermediate history), `waiting` carries `blocked: true`, every door into `done` is guarded by `require-comment`, and — the tripwire — every one-hop transition the seed relies on carries **zero** rules, because the seed submits a bare `ticket.transitioned` with no comment or evidence and any rule there would make the real engine refuse the seed's own event at ingest. This last check is what reddens if a copy of the template ever grows a rule on one of the two paths (`later`→`waiting`, `next`→`waiting`) that Ruling P29 deliberately leaves rule-free — a test pins `adviseWorkflowDef`'s two `rule-bypassable` advisories on the real template by identity, proves a single-edge "fix" genuinely silences one advisory while `checkErrandTemplate` still reddens, and proves guarding both edges clears the advisory panel to zero while `checkErrandTemplate` still refuses it. `workflowFromTemplate` clones a template into a live, published-shape definition at version 1. `apps/server/src/seed/templates.ts`
- ⚙️ **Convenience Corner seed mapping — frozen record in, events out, nothing invented** — `readCcBoard` reads the frozen `reference/convenience-corner-tracker/board.data.js` snapshot read-only through a `vm` sandbox and refuses (naming the path) a file that does not define `window.CC_TRACKER` with the expected shape. `buildErrandEvents` maps one tracker item to its full ticket event sequence: `ticket.created`, a literal-text `description`, an optional `counterparty` custom field, an origin-dated `startDate` claim (built-in field, `{date, note?}` shape) only when the record itself dates it, a `dueDate` claim derived from `needed_week` by the tracker generator's own published week arithmetic with the derivation named in the claim's note, and at most one `ticket.transitioned` (payload key `to`) mapping the tracker's `later/next/in-progress/blocked` states onto the template's `later/next/in-progress/waiting`. Refuses — never guesses or fabricates — on a `done` item (checked by both the state string and the done flag, so the two disagreeing can never slip a completed item through as open) and on any unmapped tracker state. Every event id is a `seedUlid` derived from the item's own tracker id, so two builds of the same item are identical and a re-snapshot cannot collide event ids across items. `apps/server/src/seed/{cc-board,cc-map}.ts`
- ✅ **Convenience Corner seed runner — the shop's real errands through the product's own door** — `seedConvenienceCorner` founds the **Convenience Corner** workspace, the **Shop operations** project, the **Errand** ticket type (one custom field, `counterparty`; `startDate` and `dueDate` are built-ins and need no declaration) on a clone of the errand-with-waits template, the two epic tickets **Registrations** and **Open questions** titled verbatim from the snapshot, and the 23 shop errands with their bodies, dates and states — 118 events, every one submitted through `ingestClientBatch`, the **gated client door** a person's own action goes through. The seed is handed only that door (`SeedIngest = Pick<IngestService,'ingestClientBatch'>`), so reaching for the ungated internal one is a compile error; envelope-origin is `'web'`, the founding sequence is the server's own and is never re-emitted, and any result other than committed/already-present fails the run loudly, naming the refusal. The accountant is staged: `--allow-missing-guest` seeds everything but the guest surface, and a later run — once the accountant has signed in — adds exactly the guest membership plus explicit visibility on the four errands whose record names them, with the owner kept on every explicit list. Idempotent by construction: every event id is a deterministic `seedUlid`, the runner asks the ledger which ids it already holds and submits only the rest, so a second run commits nothing and a partial first run resumes cleanly. CLI `apps/server/src/seed/cli-seed-convenience-corner.ts` (`--owner-email` / `SEED_OWNER_EMAIL`, `--accountant-email` / `SEED_ACCOUNTANT_EMAIL`, `--allow-missing-guest`, `--board-path`), wired as `pnpm --filter @gisik-nokma/server run seed:cc`. `apps/server/src/seed/{seed-convenience-corner,cli-seed-convenience-corner}.ts`
- ⚙️ **What the client door costs a seed, recorded** — a dogfooding seed meets the refusals a person meets, and two of them are not in the plan text. The owner must **join their own project** (`project.member_added`, `projectAdmin: true`) before any `ticket.created`: `checkVisibilityPermission` requires the actor be on the target project and there is deliberately no admin shortcut. And a `ticket-type.defined` is filed against a **fresh entity ULID** with the stable type key in the payload — `entitySchema` admits a non-ULID entity id for `membership` alone. Both are in `seed-convenience-corner.ts` with the measured refusal quoted beside them.
- ⚙️ **`readPlanSeries` — the plan documents are the record** — reads `docs/superpowers/plans/`, skipping plan 00 (the coordination doc — an interface authority, not an execution plan, with no `**Goal:**` line of its own), and returns each plan's verbatim H1 title, `**Goal:**` line, and every `### Task N:` heading, refusing (naming the file) a plan with no H1 rather than inventing one. `apps/server/src/seed/plan-series.ts`
- ✅ **Gisik Nokma dev seed — the plan series, seeded into the product's own build** — `seedDevWorkspace` founds one **Build — the plan series** project inside the `Gisik Nokma` workspace (founded by the bare `workspace.created` event alone; Plan 4's `bootstrapWorkspace` supplies the built-in `epic`/`task` types and workflow for free, so this seed never re-emits a `workflow.defined` or `ticket-type.defined` of its own), one **epic** ticket per plan document and one **task** ticket per `### Task N:` heading, titled verbatim from `readPlanSeries`. Same client-door discipline as Task 9's Convenience Corner seed: submitted only through `ingestClientBatch` (`CcSeedDeps`/`SeedReport` reused, not redefined), `origin: 'web'`, the owner joins their own project before any `ticket.created`, and every event id is a deterministic `seedUlid` so a second run commits nothing. Deliberately claims no dates and no transitions — the plan series has no dates of its own, and execution state belongs to the humans doing the work, not to a seed script. CLI `apps/server/src/seed/cli-seed-dev-workspace.ts` (`--owner-email` / `SEED_OWNER_EMAIL`, `--plans-dir`, default `docs/superpowers/plans`), wired as `pnpm --filter @gisik-nokma/server run seed:dev`. `apps/server/src/seed/{seed-dev-workspace,cli-seed-dev-workspace}.ts`
- ⚙️ **CI pipeline split and server-suite sharding** — the single `gate` job became eight: `scope` (the
  docs-only/full-gate decision, unchanged), `check-env` (also runs the skipIf-coverage trip-wire, below),
  `check-tasks` (the task-ledger gate, wired as its own job for the first time — previously absent from CI
  entirely, so a ledger-only PR's green verified nothing about the ledger), `build`, `typecheck`,
  `server-tests` (the 90-file server suite sharded 4-way via vitest's own `--shard`), `other-tests` (every
  other package), and `e2e` (checks for Plan 11 Task 13's one known artifact, `docker-compose.dev.yml`,
  but does NOT attempt to run anything even once it exists — see the case below for why). A `gate` job
  still fans all of them in as the one check name the auto-merge precondition reads, tolerating a
  dependency's `skipped` (the docs-only case) but not any other non-success result.
  `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
- ⚙️ **Silent-skip guard for gated test suites, plus a coverage trip-wire for where it doesn't reach** —
  `check-no-silent-skips.mjs` reads a shard's vitest JSON report and fails if any file using `.skipIf(`
  contributed zero passed-or-failed tests — the case where a runtime guard (a missing binary, a missing
  env var, or a guarded loop over an empty fixture list) eats an entire suite while the run still exits 0.
  It is wired into `server-tests` only; `check-skipif-coverage.mjs` closes the gap that leaves open — it
  fails the (cheap, no-build) `check-env` job if any `.skipIf(` usage appears outside `apps/server/test/`,
  the one place the guard actually runs, converting an uncovered guard into a loud CI failure rather than
  a silent one. Wired ahead of its first real trigger: no file in this repo uses `.skipIf(` yet; Plan 11
  Tasks 11/12 will add one around the restore-drill suite. `scripts/check-no-silent-skips.mjs`,
  `scripts/check-skipif-coverage.mjs`
- ⚙️ **DigitalOcean deploy spec (file only, not applied)** — `deploy/do/app.yaml`: server, web and a
  backup worker on DigitalOcean App Platform (region `blr`), managed Postgres attached, object storage
  and backup env vars named against the ACTUAL schema (`OBJECT_STORE_S3_*`, not the plan's original
  `S3_*` draft, which nothing reads; `SERVER_ORIGIN`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL`, not the plan's
  `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_WS_URL`, which nothing reads either), two sibling domains
  (`pm.artsourced.in`, `pm-api.artsourced.in`) routed by `ingress.rules`. `DEPLOY_SHA` is deliberately
  ABSENT rather than set to a placeholder — `/healthz` will serve it verbatim on both its 200 and 503
  branches once Lane E's work lands, but a static spec can't keep it current per-deploy, and an explicit
  `dev` value would read as a real one; the actual gap (a CI step that patches it and calls
  `doctl apps update`) is named in `deploy/do/README.md` as living in `ci.yml` (Task 14) but not built —
  itself a live-deploy action, out of scope for both tasks. `README.md` also documents provisioning by
  hand and names a `sslmode=require`-vs-`verify-full` downgrade risk found in cross-lane review (DO's
  connection string can't be rewritten by this file; the fix, if any, belongs wherever `pg`'s major
  version gets decided). Nothing in this bullet has been run against a live DigitalOcean account.
  `deploy/do/app.yaml`, `deploy/do/README.md`
- ⚙️ **Deployed-database timezone assertion** — `scripts/check-db-timezone.mjs` connects with a bare
  `pg.Client` that sets no startup options and issues no `SET TIME ZONE`, so a pass proves the
  **database's own default** is `Asia/Kolkata`, not merely that the app pool asked for it (which a
  connection pooler can silently drop). Verified falsifiable against a throwaway database explicitly
  `ALTER`ed to `UTC` before being trusted — the shared dev Postgres's cluster-wide `-c
  timezone=Asia/Kolkata` GUC makes every ordinary database (including the admin `postgres` db) answer
  IST regardless of any per-database `ALTER`, which is exactly the unfalsifiable-demo trap the plan's own
  suggested proof text would have fallen into. `scripts/check-db-timezone.mjs`, root `package.json`'s
  `check:db-tz` script
- ⚙️ **Backups — a nightly `pg_dump`, a weekly ledger export, and a daemon that phones home when
  either fails (Task 11)** — `exportLedger` writes the whole ledger as gzipped ndjson, one envelope per
  line, in `readLedger`'s canonical replay order, to `ledger/YYYY-MM-DD.ndjson.gz`. **This export, not
  the dump, is the fundamental recovery artifact**: every projection rebuilds from the ledger (Plan 1's
  rebuild path and the verify-projections gate prove it continuously), so the ndjson alone is
  sufficient. **Known ceiling, recorded rather than discovered:** `readLedger` materialises the whole
  `events` table before a byte is written, so this job's peak memory scales with the ledger even though
  its gzip/upload half is streamed — the multipart-streaming claim below is true of `pg_dump` and not of
  this. On the `basic-xxs` (512 MB) worker the deploy spec provisions, it is the one job whose OOM means
  no recovery artifact (case 13.34). `dumpDatabaseToSink` streams a custom-format `pg_dump` straight to
  `pg/YYYY-MM-DDTHH-mm.dump` without buffering it — the *convenient* restore — and on a non-zero exit it
  **discards the key it was writing to** before throwing, because the sink was handed `child.stdout`
  before the exit code existed and a truncated archive at a normal-looking dated key is what a restore
  drill reaching for "the latest dump" would pick up. Its connection password travels in `PGPASSWORD`,
  never in argv, where any process on the host could read it. `msUntilNextUtc` computes the delay to the
  next `HH:MM` UTC, optionally constrained to a day-of-week, and `startBackupDaemon` runs both jobs on
  that clock, writing **every** failure to stderr as structured JSON *itself* and then handing it to an
  `ErrorAlerter`, and re-arming rather than dying, because a backup that fails silently is a lie about
  being backed up. The stderr line lives in the daemon and not in the alerter on purpose: every alerter
  the daemon can be handed may be silent (Task 3's `NoopErrorAlerter` is empty, and the default
  deployment gets it), so an alerter-only witness would have gone quiet the moment the shim below was
  retired. `backup-daemon-main.ts` is the worker entry `deploy/do/app.yaml` names (`node
  dist/ops/backup-daemon-main.js`, confirmed against `tsconfig.json`'s `rootDir`/`outDir`); `--once` runs
  **both** jobs to completion — ledger export first, neither able to veto the other — reports both
  outcomes and exits non-zero if either failed, which is what the manual/runbook backup uses; `SIGTERM`
  stops the schedule and ends the pool.
  `apps/server/src/ops/{schedule,export-ledger,pg-dump,backup-daemon-main}.ts`, `apps/server/package.json`'s
  `ops:backup-once` script
- ⚙️ **The scheduler generalised to N jobs, and two more Plan 11 "ops errands" registered on it** —
  `startScheduler(jobs: ScheduledJob[], alerter)` extracts the daemon's private `loop`/`onFailure`
  primitive above into a standalone function over an arbitrary list of `{name, run, delayMs}` jobs, arming
  every job's first timer SYNCHRONOUSLY at construction and re-arming each independently after it settles —
  one job's failure or schedule never touches another's. `startBackupDaemon` becomes a thin wrapper over it
  with an unchanged external signature, so its own existing tests (the stderr shape, the
  `` `backup:${name}` `` alerter key, the two-timer construction) pin the wrapper byte-for-byte with no
  edits — but **`backup-daemon-main.ts` itself now calls `startScheduler` directly with all four jobs**,
  not `startBackupDaemon`: one scheduler, one `stop()`, one `'backup daemon scheduled'` log line, for the
  two backup jobs and the two errands below alike. `startBackupDaemon`'s only remaining caller is its own
  test file, kept because it is what pins `startScheduler`'s stderr/alerter contract in one place; a
  `sweep-orphans` failure in production alerts through that SAME contract, as `` `backup:sweep-orphans` ``
  with `msg: 'backup job failed'` (cases 13.25/13.27's naming, extended to the two new jobs by construction
  rather than by a fifth case). `backup-daemon-main.ts` puts two more jobs on the SAME scheduler as the
  nightly dump and weekly export: **`sweep-orphans`**, running `sweepOrphanObjects` against the worker's own object store on a
  fixed 30-day interval (first run 30 days after boot; "monthly" in the calendar sense would need real new
  day-of-month logic this maintenance sweep does not need enough to justify) and logging COUNTS only, never
  the swept key list, at `info` level; and **`sweep-notifications`**, running the existing
  `runNotificationSweep` on `NOTIFICATION_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS` (optional, default one hour — unset or empty
  both mean "use the default," the same equivalence `ALERT_WEBHOOK_URL || null` already draws elsewhere in
  this file, because an unset App Platform binding resolves to `''`). Neither new job runs under `--once`
  — that flag is "run the two BACKUP jobs right now," and folding an unrecoverable delete into it silently
  would surprise whoever reaches for it expecting only a backup — and `requirePositiveIntervalMs`, beside
  `msUntilNextUtc`, REFUSES rather than clamps a non-positive, non-finite or non-integer interval, naming
  the bad value, so a mistyped variable exits the worker 1 on its first `delayMs()` call rather than
  spinning it at 100% of a core or never firing at all. Construction is ALL-OR-NOTHING: `startScheduler`
  now calls every job's `delayMs()` up front, before arming any of their timers, so a later job's refusal
  (review-round fix, 2026-09-06) leaves zero timers live rather than orphaning the earlier, correctly-
  configured jobs' timers with no `stop()` ever returned to cancel them.
  `apps/server/src/ops/{schedule,backup-daemon-main}.ts`
- ⚙️ **The backup object sink — a second, deliberately separate S3 client, and why it is not the
  attachment one** — `s3TargetFromEnv`/`s3SinkFromEnv` build a near-put-only `ObjectSink` (multipart via
  `@aws-sdk/lib-storage`, so a multi-gigabyte dump never lands in the worker's memory; plus a `discard`
  that aborts an in-flight upload and deletes the key, so a failed dump leaves nothing behind — abort and
  delete are both needed, because an unaborted multipart upload leaves paid-for parts no listing shows)
  and `fetch-backup.ts` is its read half, pulling one object down for a restore. It reads the **live**
  `OBJECT_STORE_S3_*` credential names — the plan's own `S3_ENDPOINT`/`S3_ACCESS_KEY` draft names
  nothing reads are not used — and takes only its BUCKET from a separate variable (`BACKUP_S3_BUCKET`),
  so backups can never land in the attachment bucket. `deploy/do/app.yaml`'s backup worker recorded that
  reuse as an **UNRESOLVED** assumption and this settles it, amending that file in the same change set:
  the worker now sets endpoint, region and path-style alongside the credential pair. It has to.
  `s3TargetFromEnv` REFUSES to boot without `OBJECT_STORE_S3_ENDPOINT` and `OBJECT_STORE_S3_REGION`,
  naming them — **stricter than `createObjectStoreFromEnv`, deliberately**, not a mirror of it. An
  earlier revision required only bucket and credentials and defaulted the region to `us-east-1`, and
  that was measured to resolve `https://s3.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/gisik-nokma-backups` from the literal
  environment the worker supplied: DO Spaces credentials posted at Amazon, nightly, invisible in dev
  because `.env.example` sets both for MinIO. A backup destination has no local-disk fallback to degrade
  to, so an unset one is a boot-time refusal rather than a default pointing at another provider. It
  deliberately does **not**
  reuse `attachments/object-store.ts`'s `assertValidObjectKey`: that validator refuses any key
  containing a dot and is right to, because attachment keys derive from caller-supplied input where a
  `..` is a traversal attempt (its own planted-defect test pins that). Backup keys are server-generated
  from a timestamp and legitimately carry dots, so widening a live security control in one subsystem to
  suit another was rejected in favour of two separate implementations — the reasoning is recorded in the
  file so nobody "fixes" it back. Every credential is destructured off `env` rather than read as
  `env.NAME`, the one shape `scripts/check-env-example.mjs`'s D2 detector can see — load-bearing for a
  future backup-only variable rather than for these five, which the gate already attributes to
  `attachments/store-config.ts` (its `record()` is first-write-wins and the walker reaches `attachments/`
  first); proven by adding a genuinely new name to this file's destructuring and watching the gate fail
  naming this path (case 13.20).
  `apps/server/src/ops/{object-sink,fetch-backup}.ts`, `apps/server/package.json`'s `ops:fetch-backup`
  script
- ⚙️ **~~`error-alerter-shim.ts` — a named placeholder, not a second implementation~~ RETIRED AND
  DELETED in this PR, exactly as its own header instructed.** Task 3 landed here (seam 1), so
  `src/ops/schedule.ts` and `src/ops/backup-daemon-main.ts` now import `../alerts/error-alerter.js`
  directly and the shim file is gone. The swap was safe by type identity, not by hope: the real module
  exports `type ErrorAlerter = { notify(context, error): Promise<void> }` and
  `alerterFromConfig(alertWebhookUrl: string | null): ErrorAlerter` — the exact pair the shim mirrored.
  The never-silent property does NOT come back with it and never did: it lives in `startBackupDaemon`'s
  own catch (cases 13.25–13.27), deliberately moved out of the shim before the swap, because the real
  `WebhookErrorAlerter` POSTs without logging and `NoopErrorAlerter.notify()` is empty. **The sentence
  below is struck through rather than deleted because it was the reason the shim was correct to write,
  and a reader who finds the shim referenced elsewhere needs it.** ~~Task 3's real
  `apps/server/src/alerts/error-alerter.ts` is unmerged, so this file exports the same two names
  (`ErrorAlerter`, `alerterFromConfig`) with the same shapes, logs every failure as structured JSON to
  stderr regardless, and posts best-effort to `ALERT_WEBHOOK_URL` — catching and logging a send failure~~
  rather than letting the failure to report a failure become a second failure. Writing `src/alerts/`
  here would have landed a competing implementation the moment Task 3 merges; the file's header says so
  and names the one-line change (delete it, repoint two imports) that retires it. That change really is
  one line now: the never-silent property lives in `startBackupDaemon`'s own catch, not here, so
  swapping in Task 3's logging-free `WebhookErrorAlerter` or empty `NoopErrorAlerter` cannot make a
  backup failure silent.
  `apps/server/src/ops/error-alerter-shim.ts`
- ⚙️ **The restore drill — `restore.sh`'s scratch-only guard, and the automated proof that a restored
  database still verifies (Task 12)** — `scripts/restore.sh <dump-file> <target-database-url>
  [--force-live]` runs `pg_restore --no-owner --no-privileges` and then prints the `verify:projections`
  command, because **a restore is not done until the freshness gate is clean** — the script deliberately
  does not claim success on its own. Its guard reads the database name out of the target URL and
  **refuses any name not containing `scratch`, `restore` or `drill`** unless `--force-live` is passed:
  restoring over a live database is a deliberate act, not a typo away. The guard runs BEFORE the
  dump-file existence check, so a mistyped target is answered with the target, not sent looking for a
  file. `apps/server/test/ops/restore-drill.test.ts` dumps a seeded database, restores into a bare
  scratch database and accepts the restore only when **the row count crossed** (the restored `events`
  count matches the count measured in the source, with a `> 0` floor) **and** `verifyProjections` is
  clean **and** the restored ledger still refuses an UPDATE; a planted-drift case corrupts
  `proj_entity_heads` and requires the gate to name that table. The count check is not decoration:
  measured by restoring `--schema-only`, the verify-clean case still PASSED against an empty database
  while the count case failed `expected +0 to be 21` — an empty structure verifies clean, so verify
  alone could never have proven a restore restored anything. The script itself is executed by the suite
  rather than described by it — refusal, the same invocation one word different in the name getting past
  the guard, `--force-live`, the usage text, and a real happy-path restore into a second scratch database
  whose row count is then read back from the database.
  **For a URL-shaped DSN, the password never reaches argv or the terminal.** `restore.sh` splits it in bash — the
  same split `src/ops/pg-dump.ts`'s `splitDatabaseUrl` performs for the daemon (case 13.31), including
  the percent-decode, so the two halves of the pair agree on one DSN — and hands the credential to libpq
  through `PGPASSWORD`. `pg_restore` is invoked with a password-free URL, and the `verify:projections`
  next-step line it prints is password-free too, with a note telling the operator to re-supply it. This
  is an operator script run on the ops box during an incident, where argv is world-readable (`ps -ef`)
  and terminal scrollback outlives the incident. Both halves are asserted, and the exit-0 restore beside
  them is what shows the credential was REROUTED rather than merely deleted. **The stated limit:** a DSN
  the split cannot parse — libpq's non-URL conninfo form, `host=... password=...` — falls through
  untouched and keeps the old argv behaviour. That is the same fail-open-on-parse choice
  `splitDatabaseUrl` makes, for the same reason (refusing would break a form the tool accepts), and it is
  a real remaining exposure for anyone who restores with a conninfo string rather than a URL.
  **Version rule, and the one thing here NO automated test covers:** the `pg_restore` used must be at
  least as new as the `pg_dump` that produced the archive AND no newer than the target server's major
  version. Measured 2026-09-06: `pg_restore` 18.4 opens every restore with `SET transaction_timeout = 0`
  (PG17+), which a 16 server rejects — the data lands and the exit code is still non-zero. The rule is
  printed by `restore.sh` on the path that actually restores (not only by `usage()`, which an operator
  who typed the command correctly never sees), and stated in `deploy/do/README.md`.
  **Skew is the ONLY thing allowed to skip the round trip.** The suite gates on client-major ≤
  server-major, not merely on the binaries existing, and skips loudly (naming both majors) on a skewed
  workstation rather than reddening; CI runs it on the matched pair `ci.yml` already pins
  (`postgres:16-alpine` + `postgresql-client-16`). Every OTHER reason a version probe might come back
  empty — no `psql`, no libpq on `PATH`, Postgres down or not yet ready, unparseable output — is a
  **failure**, asserted by an always-running precondition test that names the probe and quotes its error.
  It has to be: the probes used to fail open, `check-no-silent-skips.mjs` cannot see a partially skipped
  file (the four ungated guard cases always contribute passes), and a readiness blip in CI would
  otherwise have turned the whole round trip — count, verify, and the only execution of `restore.sh`'s
  happy path — into a green skip. The trade is disclosed: a machine with no libpq at all now reddens
  here where it used to skip. The guard cases need only bash and are ungated, so they run everywhere.
  `scripts/restore.sh`, `apps/server/test/ops/restore-drill.test.ts`, `deploy/do/README.md`
- ⚙️ **Deployment config surface, CA-aware pool, versioned `/healthz` (Task 1)** — `loadConfig` grows
  nine deployment knobs (`WEB_ORIGIN`, `TRUST_PROXY`, `RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX`, `RATE_LIMIT_EVENTS_MAX`,
  `RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_SEC`, `ALERT_WEBHOOK_URL`, `SMTP_URL`, `MAGIC_LINK_FROM`, `DATABASE_CA_CERT`) plus a
  tenth not in the plan, `DEPLOY_SHA`, every one refusing loudly by name via `ConfigError`.
  `WEB_ORIGIN` shipped **required, with no default** — a deviation from the plan's optional
  `string | null` — because it is the host of every invitation link. `httpUrl()`/`smtpUrl()` close a
  real bug: `z.string().url()` accepts `localhost:3001` (the WHATWG parser reads `localhost:` as a
  scheme), which would have minted unfollowable links. `createPool(databaseUrl, tls)` strips six `ssl*`
  query parameters before attaching an explicit `ssl: { ca: caCert }`, because `pg` otherwise
  `Object.assign`s the parsed connection string over the caller's `ssl` option — every DigitalOcean
  managed-Postgres URL carries `?sslmode=require` and would silently discard a configured CA under the
  unmodified library behaviour. `/healthz` answers `{ status, sha }` — **not `{ ..., version }`** as the
  plan specified — on both its 200 and 503 branches (Ruling P33), `sha` from `DEPLOY_SHA` defaulting to
  `'dev'`. **Two defects were found and deliberately left open, recorded rather than fixed:** an
  `options=` query parameter still clobbers the pool's timezone startup parameter through the same
  `Object.assign` (asserted as present, not hidden); and `sslcert`/`sslkey`/`sslrootcert` cause `pg` to
  `readFileSync` at client construction, turning a missing file into an `ENOENT` at pool creation — this
  one has no test at all. `apps/server/src/config.ts`, `apps/server/src/db.ts`, `apps/server/src/app.ts`
- ⚙️ **CORS, three rate-limit buckets, and an HTTP-Origin (CSRF) check (Task 2)** — `httpOriginRefused()`
  refuses a `POST` to one of seven `HTTP_ORIGIN_PROTECTED_ROUTES` only when the request carries a
  `Cookie` header (an ambient credential) and `Origin` does not match normalized `WEB_ORIGIN`; matched
  against the route **pattern** (`request.routeOptions.url`), not the raw URL, so a query string cannot
  buy a bypass. `makeFixedWindowLimiter`/`rateLimitKeyFor` key rate limiting on the **rightmost**
  `X-Forwarded-For` entry, not `request.ip` — the plan did not specify a keying strategy, and the obvious
  one (leftmost, under `trustProxy: true`) is attacker-choosable; this was caught and fixed before
  shipping. Three independent buckets — `authMax`, `eventsMax`, and an **`inviteMax` not in the plan's
  two-bucket design**, added because invite-issuance mints a credential per request and had no bucket
  under either of the plan's two; `POST /v1/attachments`, previously unlimited, now shares the `events`
  budget. `webOrigin: null` turns off CORS **and** the CSRF check together, by design — no independent
  switch. Three routes (`/v1/workspaces`, the ticket-type migrate route, mark-notification-read) were
  found missing from an initial "exhaustive" protected-route list by forging a request at every
  non-GET route in the assembled table, not by re-reading the list; the suite now asserts set-equality
  between the protected-route table and the production list so that drift reddens. **Not covered:**
  `rateLimitKeyFor` has no standalone unit test, only indirect HTTP-integration coverage; whether
  `apps/web`'s proxy actually forwards `Origin` on same-origin requests is undemonstrated in this repo.
  `apps/server/src/http/http-origin.ts`, `apps/server/src/http/rate-limit.ts`, `apps/server/src/app.ts`
- ⚙️ **Error alerting hook on 5xx, contained against its own failure modes (Task 3)** — `setErrorHandler`
  lets sub-500 errors pass through unchanged; a 5xx is logged, alerted via `alertWithoutWaiting` with the
  request URL redacted (`redactInviteToken`, reused from the invites module, so an unspent invitation
  token never lands in a chat webhook), and answered with the fixed `INTERNAL_ERROR_BODY` — **not** the
  plan's `{ error: 'internal_error' }` shape. `alertWithoutWaiting` exists because the plan's own wiring
  snippet (`void alerter.notify(...)`, no try, no catch) would let a promise rejection float and end the
  process on default Node settings — "the monitoring hook becoming the outage" — so it carries both a
  `try` (for a synchronous throw) and a `.catch` (for a rejection), proven by a real
  `process.on('unhandledRejection', …)` collector asserting nothing escapes either way, and it never
  waits for the alert to settle before the response is sent. `WebhookErrorAlerter` POSTs `{ text }` with
  a 5s `AbortSignal` timeout and swallows its own failures. **Deliberately NOT built, against the plan's
  Step 3:** process-level `unhandledRejection`/`uncaughtException` handlers — installing them would
  suppress Node's own crash-and-restart behaviour; the one narrow exception (active only during a
  shutdown, for a synchronously-throwing close hook) is a later, separate commit
  (`installShutdownHandlers`, above). **`/healthz`'s 503 branch deliberately never reaches this
  alerter** — the database-down case, named in prose as exactly the failure an on-call webhook exists
  for — and nothing here coalesces repeated failures into one page. Neither boundary is guarded by a
  test; both are asserted only in source comments. **This is the real implementation Lane C's
  `error-alerter-shim.ts` bullet (above) was written against as still-unmerged** — it has since landed on
  this branch, so that shim is now retirable by the one-line change its own file header already names
  (delete it, repoint two imports). `apps/server/src/alerts/error-alerter.ts`,
  `apps/server/src/app.ts`, `apps/server/src/index.ts`
- ⚙️ **SMTP magic-link sender, and phone/OTP sign-in refused rather than logged (Task 4)** —
  `SmtpMagicLinkSender` sends a real, **plain-text-only** magic-link email via nodemailer (subject
  `MAGIC_LINK_SUBJECT`), constructed with explicit 10s connect / 10s greeting / 20s socket timeouts —
  nodemailer's defaults (2 min / 30 s / 10 min) would hold a sign-in HTTP request open for minutes
  against an unreachable mail host, since sending is awaited inline; measured with a real TCP listener
  that accepts and never speaks, asserting rejection bounded both above and below the configured
  timeout. `NullOtpSender` **refuses** every OTP send with a 501 (`PHONE_SIGN_IN_UNAVAILABLE`) —
  a bigger, security-relevant deviation from the plan, which asked for `OtpSender` to stay wired to the
  dev-only `LogOtpSender` (silently logging codes) in production; `selectOtpSender()` is asserted to
  never return `LogOtpSender`. `selectMagicLinkSender`/`selectOtpSender` (`select-senders.ts`, not in
  the plan at all) extract the choice into pure, config-only functions so it is unit-testable outside
  `main()`, which opens a Postgres pool on its first line. Verified over the real mounted route
  (`app.inject` against `/api/auth/phone-number/send-otp`): the 501 body is exactly `{code, message}`
  with nothing extra, and the identical request against `LogOtpSender` (a positive control) still
  succeeds, ruling out a broken route as the explanation for the 501. **Not covered:** whether
  `requireTLS` should be forced on the SMTP transport (deliberately left off, to keep working against
  Mailpit's plaintext `smtp://`); that a refused OTP request still leaves an expiring verification row
  in better-auth's own storage (documented as an accepted side effect, not tested).
  `apps/server/src/auth/{smtp-magic-link-sender,otp-sender,select-senders}.ts`
- ⚙️ **Server and web Dockerfiles (Task 5)** — `apps/server/Dockerfile`: multi-stage `node:22-alpine`,
  `pnpm deploy --prod` then **`rm -rf /out/src /out/test`** before the runtime `COPY` (not in the plan's
  snippet — `pnpm deploy --prod` prunes devDependencies but not the package's own source files, absent a
  `files` field), migrations run inline in the `ENTRYPOINT` shell string rather than the plan's separate
  `docker-entrypoint.sh`, which was never created. `apps/web/Dockerfile`: `output: 'standalone'`, an
  empty `public/` created at build time (the app ships none). A build-arg defect shipped and was fixed
  the same day: the plan's `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_WS_URL` have zero readers anywhere in
  `apps/`/`packages/`, and an unset `SERVER_ORIGIN` bakes `http://localhost:3000` into
  `routes-manifest.json` at build time, routing the proxy at itself instead of the server; replaced with
  `SERVER_ORIGIN`/`NEXT_PUBLIC_SERVER_URL`, the names the web app actually reads (regression coverage
  for this specific bug lives in Task 6's compose contract, below, not here). **Manually observed, not
  automated:** both images build (server ~607 MB, web ~241 MB standalone); the server image run with no
  database prints "applying migrations…" then dies `ECONNREFUSED`. **Neither image has been run against
  a real Postgres, and `node apps/web/server.js` has never answered a request** — no automated test
  builds or runs either Dockerfile. `apps/server/Dockerfile`, `apps/web/Dockerfile`,
  `apps/web/next.config.ts`, root `.dockerignore`
- ⚙️ **Dev stack (`docker-compose.dev.yml`) checked against the real config loaders, not eyeballed
  (Task 6)** — six services (`postgres`, `minio`, `minio-init`, `mailpit`, `server`, `web`) under an
  explicit project `name: gisik-nokma-dev`. A load-bearing bug shipped and was fixed the same day: the
  first version and the root `docker-compose.yml` both resolved to the same (directory-inferred) Compose
  project name and both declared a service literally named `postgres`, so bringing either stack up
  **destroyed the other's containers** — directly contradicting the commit's own "the two stacks are now
  co-runnable" claim. The suite calls the **real** `loadConfig()` and `createObjectStoreFromEnv()`
  against the parsed `server` service env and asserts neither throws and the store resolves to
  `S3ObjectStore` (not the local-disk fallback) — upgraded from a `toBeTruthy()` key-presence check
  after review showed that passed against a malformed `DATABASE_URL`, a schemeless `BETTER_AUTH_URL`,
  and a mismatched bucket name. Object-store vars are `OBJECT_STORE_S3_*`, not the plan's bare `S3_*` —
  nothing reads the plan's names (0 hits against a positive control of 22 for the correct ones); the
  "no bare `S3_*` keys" check runs through an `envOf()` helper that normalizes both Compose environment
  syntaxes, because list-form `Object.keys()` yielding `'0','1','2'` made an earlier version of that
  exact check pass vacuously against a file that still had three. Postgres is published on 54330, not
  the plan's 54329 (the live shared cross-lane test Postgres) — asserted both ways, since a
  negative-only check would pass if the ports block vanished entirely. `scripts/check-env-example.mjs`
  was reused, **not created by this task** — it already existed from an earlier lane's work. **The
  suite's own docblock is explicit: this is not the Task 6 acceptance.** The acceptance is a request
  travelling through the web container's proxy and being answered by the server, live — nothing
  automated brings the stack up and makes that round trip; it is a hand-run case. `docker-compose.dev.yml`,
  `apps/server/test/deploy/compose-dev-contract.test.ts`
- ✅ **Deployed-stack E2E — Playwright against the running compose stack, and the sign-in defect it
  found** — three specs drive the REAL stack (a Next container proxying to a real API server, real
  Postgres, real Mailpit, real MinIO): sign-in through the shipped form with the magic link fetched out of
  the test inbox, the seeded Convenience Corner board rendered from real projections, `POST /v1/events`
  through the web container's proxy carrying the session cookie the browser earned, and a guest seeing the
  workspace but NOT its projects — ruling P38's partial workspace, seen from a browser for the first time.
  Run with `pnpm run test:e2e:stack` against a stack that is already up. It lives under a **second**
  Playwright config (`apps/web/playwright.stack.config.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/`) with **no `webServer`**,
  because the existing mock-only smoke's config starts `pnpm dev` on 3001 with `reuseExistingServer` — so
  real-stack specs under it would either collide with the compose web container or silently adopt a stray
  dev server and report deployment coverage of a deployment they never touched. It is off the gate
  **structurally**: `test:e2e:stack` is a script name turbo defines no task for (measured before and
  after: 16 tasks, 10 `#test`, zero e2e), which matters because playwright declares no install script, so
  the browser binary is an explicit CI step and nowhere else. `global-setup.ts` REFUSES the run unless what
  is listening is really the stack — a proxied `/v1` request must be answered by the API server, with the
  server's own error body, inside a time bound — and that assertion has a measured negative control rather
  than an argued one: an image built without `SERVER_ORIGIN` (what `up -d` reuses without `--build`)
  proxies `/v1/*` at itself and answers **500 after 30 s**, while `docker compose ps` reports every
  container healthy in both states. The Mailpit helper purges the inbox immediately before each send and
  then requires **exactly one** message, because `messages[0]` under an unknown sort order can return an
  already-spent link and following one does not fail loudly — the earlier session cookie is still in the
  context. `apps/web/e2e-stack/*`, `apps/web/playwright.stack.config.ts`, the `e2e` job in
  `.github/workflows/ci.yml`
- ⚙️ **`WEB_ORIGIN` is a trusted auth origin — the product no longer refuses its own client** —
  better-auth's `trustedOrigins` was left at its default, the origin of `BETTER_AUTH_URL`, which is the
  SERVER's origin and not what any browser sends; a real browser at the web origin submitting the shipped
  sign-in form was answered `403 INVALID_ORIGIN` with "Invalid origin" rendered on the page. The
  configured `WEB_ORIGIN` is now appended to `trustedOrigins` — **appended, never substituted**
  (`getTrustedOrigins` pushes the `baseURL` origin first and only then appends), never a wildcard, and
  never read from the request — so a same-origin deployment is unaffected and a cross-origin one works.
  `webOrigin` is a **required** field on `AuthDeps` rather than an optional one, so the typecheck names
  every caller instead of a default hiding one. The condition was not hypothetical: `deploy/do/app.yaml`
  serves the web app and the API from different hosts, so the production deploy would have met it too.
  Trusting the origin is necessary and **not sufficient** for a genuinely cross-origin deployment — the
  CORS preflight is still unmounted on the auth catch-all, which `sign-in-flows.test.ts` pins as a known
  gap. `apps/server/src/auth/auth.ts`, `apps/server/src/index.ts`, `apps/server/src/auth/auth-dev.ts`
- ⚙️ **Operator runbook — and the one thing about prose that can be made to fail** — `docs/runbook.md`
  carries deploy (routine and first-time), the three separate settings that hold Asia/Kolkata up and why
  they are not redundant, secret rotation, backups, the orphaned-upload sweep, the monthly restore drill,
  production seeding, and incident basics. **Every command it names is checked by
  `scripts/check-runbook-commands.mjs`**, wired as its own CI step: each `pnpm --filter <pkg> run
  <script>`, each root `pnpm <script>` and each repo-relative script path must resolve, and the checker
  self-tests against four planted defects before it will report a clean sweep. That is claimed as nothing
  more than it is — it proves no named command is a ghost, not that any procedure is correct. It exists
  because the first draft contained two ghosts: `ops:sweep-orphans`, which has never existed (the script
  is `sweep:orphans`), and a restore-drill step that could not run at all. `docs/runbook.md`,
  `scripts/check-runbook-commands.mjs`
- ⚙️ **`verify:projections` runs from `DATABASE_URL` alone** — it previously opened with
  `loadConfig(process.env)`, demanding `BETTER_AUTH_URL`, a 32-character `BETTER_AUTH_SECRET` and
  `WEB_ORIGIN`, none of which it reads, so the runbook's own restore drill answered `ConfigError`. A drill
  nobody can run is not a recovery procedure. It now matches the seed CLIs and `sweep-notifications`,
  which read `DATABASE_URL` directly for the same reason. **The privilege question the deploy spec owed is
  discharged as a recorded decision** in `docs/runbook.md` and `deploy/do/README.md`: the verifier runs
  only against restore scratch databases, never production, and no DDL-capable credential is added. It
  needs `CREATE SCHEMA` + `CREATE TABLE` on whatever database it verifies (it rebuilds into a
  `verify_shadow` schema), and that shadow trick is safe only while every registered projection declares
  its table — one missing from `tables` gets no shadow copy and its unqualified `TRUNCATE` resolves to
  `public`, truncating the LIVE table. It does **not** rewrite live projections; an earlier draft said it
  did, and the verifier's own docblock ("Live tables are never written") plus cases 5.55/6.117/7.63
  contradict that.
  The cost is recorded with the decision: production drift surfaces at the next monthly drill, so the
  exposure window is up to a month. `apps/server/src/tools/verify-projections.ts`, `deploy/do/README.md`
- ⚙️ **The `test-cases/` <-> test citation check (Lane F Pass 1) — the gap the coordination doc named by
  hand, made falsifiable** — a checked (`✅`) catalogue case is supposed to name the test that proves it,
  but nothing enforced the reverse: the coordination doc recorded, 2026-08-31, that no test in the repo
  cites a case id, so a `✅` could survive the deletion of its own covering test. `scripts/check-case-
  citations.mjs`, its own ungated CI job, closes the direction that was missing: every checked id in
  `test-cases/*.md` must appear as a test name or comment somewhere under a test/e2e directory. **Measured
  before choosing a mode:** 1525 checked ids, 710 already cited, 815 not — too large to backfill in this
  errand, so it runs as a RATCHET (`scripts/fixtures/check-case-citations-baseline.json`, one uncited-count
  ceiling per file) that must equal reality EXACTLY, not merely bound it: higher than reality is a
  regression (a citing test was deleted, or a new checked case shipped uncited) and fails, naming the
  offending ids; lower than reality is a STALE ceiling — also a failure, because an upper bound nobody has
  to tighten stops meaning anything and could hide a real regression on one file behind unrelated
  improvement on another if the check summed across files instead of holding each one to its own number.
  Self-tested against a planted fixture covering every id-line shape this repo's catalogues actually use
  (bare/bold/marker-first/RANGE, both before and after the id) plus a malformed-id guard (a three-part
  number like `13.2.1` is not silently truncated to a shorter real-looking id, and a cross-section range
  like `90.30–91.2` is not silently expanded or reduced to its leader), and against the ratchet's own four
  failure shapes directly. **A review round found the range shape had been silently dropping cases
  entirely**, not merely miscounting them: `- **3.89–3.94** ✅ ...` extracted only `3.89` and discarded
  the other five ids with no report at all, hiding 50 real, already-cited `✅` ids from the ratchet
  repo-wide (`03-foundation-ledger.md` alone: 10 range lines). Fixed to expand every id in a real range
  and flag a cross-section one as malformed instead. The same round found `isCited`'s boundary guard let
  a dependency version string (`"zod 3.25.76"`) satisfy a shorter id (`3.25`) with no real citation
  anywhere — fixed by also excluding a further digit after a following `.`, while still accepting an
  ordinary sentence-ending period (`case 5.18.`). The delete-a-citing-test property was also reproduced
  against REAL files, not
  only the synthetic fixture: removing both of case 11.1's citations moved `11-calendar-notifications.md`
  from 0 to 1 uncited against a committed ceiling of 0, and the check failed naming `11.1`; restored and
  re-verified clean. **Its own job carries no `needs: scope` and no docs-only `if:` gate** — the exact
  shape `check-runbook` was rewritten into after review found it silently skipped on a runbook-only diff,
  applied here before the same mistake could repeat one level up. `scripts/check-case-citations.mjs`,
  `scripts/fixtures/check-case-citations-{fixture.md,baseline.json}`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
  `test-cases/README.md`, cases 13.204–13.208
- ⚙️ **Responsive and contrast measured in real viewports, not read off the source** — two suites, deliberately separate because the interesting pages sit on opposite sides of a sign-in. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/` runs the five signed-out routes across three real device viewports (390 / 768 / 1512) under its own `next dev` on a dedicated port with `reuseExistingServer: false`, so it can never silently adopt whatever is listening on 3001 and report coverage of a container it did not start. `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts` covers the signed-in shell, because the defect that motivated all this — `.gn-nav` running 10px past a 390px viewport — was on the authenticated pages and on none of the signed-out ones. **Every case asserts the viewport actually took effect by reading `window.innerWidth` back inside the page**, since this repo has a recorded failure where a resize reported success while the page stayed 1512px wide. The load-bearing case is the cross-width one: a suite passing identically at 390 and 1512 has proven nothing about responsiveness. Tap targets ratchet against a committed baseline rather than asserting a floor — the count may fall, never rise — because that reading does not move with the viewport and is recorded as an owner decision in `docs/pre-launch-checklist.md`. `apps/web/playwright.viewport.config.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts`, cases 13.254–13.255

## 14. MCP server

*The assistant-facing door. An MCP server a person runs on their own machine, signed in as
themselves, whose writes are attributable in the ledger as an assistant's rather than a person's.
Mirrored by `test-cases/14-mcp-server.md`. The envelope-origin half of this feature lives in §3,
where the ledger's provenance vocabulary is defined, rather than being restated here.*

- ✅ **An assistant can operate gisik-nokma as you** — nine tools over stdio: five reads (`list_workspaces`, `list_projects`, `search_tickets`, `list_tickets`, `get_ticket`) and four writes (`create_ticket`, `transition_ticket`, `add_comment`, `assign_ticket`). It has no permissions of its own — every call is the call you would have made, judged by the same permission gate, rule engine and visibility rules as the web client, and every write is a real domain event through `POST /v1/events`. Driven end to end against the live stack: a ticket created at seq 1, a comment at seq 2, a bad transition refused as `refused by unknown-state: The workflow "Errand with waits" has no state "no-such-state".`, and the ledger reading `web 123 · server 6 · mcp 2`. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/`, cases 14.60–14.88

- ⚙️ **`@gisik-nokma/mcp-server` — the package scaffold and its configuration** (MCP plan Task 1) — a workspace package for the assistant-facing MCP door, with `loadMcpConfig(env, home)` as the one place its configuration is resolved: it REQUIRES `GISIK_SERVER_URL` and names it when absent, defaults the token cache to `~/.gisik-nokma/mcp-token`, and strips a trailing slash so later URL joins never double it. The env arrives as a PARAMETER rather than being read from `process.env`, which makes the three cases pure functions of their inputs — and, as a side effect nobody wanted, makes the file invisible to `pnpm check:env`, whose detectors all require the string `process.env` to appear (recorded in `.env.example`; owner: whoever next touches `scripts/check-env-example.mjs`). The package is registered with `check-runbook-commands.mjs`'s hand-maintained package list NOW, before Task 10's runbook entry exists, so that entry cannot land against a list that does not know the package. Cases 14.1–14.3. `packages/mcp-server/`
- ⚙️ **The MCP credential store — device-code grants and personal access tokens** (MCP plan Task 2) — two server-owned tables, `mcp_device_codes` and `mcp_tokens`, both hashed at rest (sha256 of 32 bytes from `randomBytes`; a plain hash rather than a password KDF is correct here for the same reason it is for `invite_tokens` — the input is 256 bits of uniform entropy, so there is no dictionary to stretch against). **Neither is a projection and neither is rebuildable**, deliberately: what they hold is absent from the ledger, because anyone who can read a workspace's history can read an event id and a payload. Neither name may ever appear in a projection's `tables` array — `reset()` empties what that array names, and a rebuild that emptied `mcp_tokens` would sign every MCP client out with no way to put them back. Single-use is enforced by `DELETE … RETURNING` in the statement that reads the grant, not by a read-shaped guard. A revoked token and a forged one resolve to `null` identically, because every distinction is an oracle. The user code uses a no-vowel, no-0/O/1/I/5/S alphabet so it can neither spell a word nor be transcribed into a different valid code. Cases 14.10–14.16. `apps/server/src/mcp/token-store.ts`, `apps/server/migrations/1788600000000_mcp-device-auth.sql`
- ⚙️ **The MCP device-authorization flow — three routes** (MCP plan Task 3) — `POST /v1/mcp/device/code` mints a grant, `POST /v1/mcp/device/approve` binds it to the signed-in person, `POST /v1/mcp/device/token` exchanges it once. **Two of the three carry no session by design**, because the point of a device flow is that the thing asking for a credential does not have one yet; neither gives anything away, since the credential is created by the APPROVAL. That approval is a cookie-authenticatable POST that mints a credential, so it is inside `HTTP_ORIGIN_PROTECTED_ROUTES` — without it, a page the victim visits could approve the ATTACKER's device code on the victim's ambient cookie, and `SameSite=Lax` does not help because the product and API are same-site siblings of one registrable domain. All three are charged to a **fourth rate-limit bucket** fed by the existing `RATE_LIMIT_AUTH_MAX` (the `inviteMax` precedent, no new environment variable): before it, a route under `/v1/mcp/...` matched no bucket at all, leaving the 8-character user code an unlimited brute-force surface. That budget is also what fixes the TTL and poll interval the spec left open. Cases 14.20–14.27b. `apps/server/src/routes/mcp-device.ts`, `apps/server/src/http/http-origin.ts`, `apps/server/src/app.ts`
- ⚙️ **A personal access token authenticates a product route — as a FALLBACK, not a branch** (MCP plan Task 4) — `requireSession` asks better-auth first, always, and consults `mcp_tokens` only when it answers null. That order is the design rather than a detail: every mobile and desktop client, and every test in this repository, authenticates with a better-auth BEARER token, so the two credential systems arrive in the same header and only the order keeps them from contending. **The order is held by a test** (14.35), not by a comment: a row keyed on the sha256 of a live session token but pointed at a different user is simultaneously a valid session for one person and a valid PAT for another, and the device-approval route makes the resolved identity observable. A forged token and a revoked one are refused identically. The cost is stated: a request that falls THROUGH to the PAT branch performs one indexed single-row write, because the lookup stamps `last_used`; a better-auth session never reaches it. Cases 14.30–14.37. `apps/server/src/auth/require-session.ts`
- ✅ **The MCP approval screen — the one place a person consents to an assistant acting as them** (MCP plan Task 5) — at `${WEB_ORIGIN}/mcp/authorize`, the `verificationUri` the device-code route hands out. It must live in `apps/web` and nowhere else, and that is measured rather than argued: an approval POST from a non-`WEB_ORIGIN` page answers **403 `forbidden_origin`**, while the same request from the real web origin passes the origin check. The consent is stated before the button and again in the confirmation — an assistant that acts **as this person**, with exactly their permissions, until revoked — and it is styled in `surface-sunken` rather than danger or warning, following the 404 page's recorded reasoning that an alarm colour tells the reader something has gone wrong when the product is behaving correctly. Dressing consent as an alarm trains people to click through alarms. Case 14.100. `apps/web/app/mcp/authorize/page.tsx`
- ⚙️ **The MCP client's token cache and HTTP layer** (MCP plan Task 6) — `readCachedToken`/`writeCachedToken`/`clearCachedToken` keep the personal access token at `0600` in a `0700` directory, with the modes passed explicitly rather than left to the process umask; a credential every process on a shared machine can read is not cached, it is published. Absent, unreadable and corrupt all answer `null`, because they mean one thing to a caller and one of them is recoverable by re-running the device flow. `createGisikClient` sends a bearer credential and **never a cookie** — a bearer-only caller is outside the HTTP-Origin check by construction — carries the server's error body verbatim rather than summarising it, and **does not retry a transport failure**, since a write retried against a server that becomes reachable mid-retry double-submits. Cases 14.40–14.47. `packages/mcp-server/src/token-cache.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/http-client.ts`
- ⚙️ **The MCP device-flow login driver and its session** (MCP plan Task 7) — `runDeviceLogin` mints a code, shows it to the person BEFORE polling (they cannot approve what they have not seen), polls at **the interval the server named** rather than a constant the client carries, and gives up at a deadline instead of forever. `createSession` uses a cached token when there is one and otherwise runs the flow; a 401 re-authenticates and retries **exactly once**, because a server answering 401 to everything would otherwise spin forever and spend the device-flow rate-limit budget doing it — and the user id moves with the token, since a stale `actorId` draws an identity-spoof refusal a long way from its cause. **A 404 never re-authenticates**: the product answers it for "does not exist" and "not yours" alike, so re-auth would turn a correct visibility refusal into a login prompt. Cases 14.50–14.57. `packages/mcp-server/src/device-login.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/session.ts`
- ✅ **Five MCP read tools** (MCP plan Task 8) — `list_workspaces`, `list_projects`, `search_tickets`, `list_tickets`, `get_ticket`, each a passthrough of a route that already exists. They do not invent routes (`list_projects` reads the workspace directory and filters client-side, because the server has no projects route), do not normalise the server's shapes (`get_ticket` returns snake_case `ticket` beside camelCase `ticketState`, untouched), and do not restate the domain's `filterSpecSchema` — which is `.strict()` with SINGULAR assignee/collaborator keys, so an array is refused rather than silently matching nothing. One rule is duplicated on purpose: a filter carrying `text` is refused before the network, because the server's `invalid_filter` does not say which of the two text terms is the problem. Cases 14.60–14.66. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/read-tools.ts`
- ✅ **Four MCP write tools, and every write is attributable as an assistant's** (MCP plan Task 9) — `create_ticket`, `transition_ticket`, `add_comment`, `assign_ticket`, each building an envelope that carries `origin: 'mcp'` (owner ruling 1) and `actorId` equal to the session user, which the permission gate requires. One write path reads the result, because `POST /v1/events` answers **200 for a refusal** — refusals ride inside `results[]`, index-aligned — so a caller checking the status alone would report every refusal as a success; the rule's name and the reason's own sentence are surfaced verbatim, since a reworded refusal is a rule the product does not have. The envelope is validated in-process by the product's own `parseEvent` and registry, so a wrong payload key fails locally rather than after a round trip. Cases 14.70–14.77. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/write-tools.ts`
- ✅ **The MCP server runs: a `gisik-nokma-mcp` stdio entrypoint, nine registered tools, and a README that says how to install and revoke it** (MCP plan Task 10) — `register.ts` is the ONE file that knows the MCP SDK's registration API (verified against the installed `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk@1.30.0`: `inputSchema` takes a zod RAW SHAPE, which `ToolDefinition` already carries, so no `zod-to-json-schema` conversion exists anywhere); everything else speaks `ToolDefinition` and every tool test runs with no transport. A handler's throw becomes `isError` with the message unchanged, so a rule-engine refusal reaches the assistant in the product's own words. **The transport connects BEFORE any credential exists** — the device flow runs on the first tool call, because authenticating first leaves `initialize` unanswered for up to ten minutes on a fresh install and the client reports a server that failed to start (measured: 107 ms and nine tools as shipped, versus an 8-second timeout with the ordering reversed). Nothing in the package may write to stdout, which is the protocol stream; the sign-in prompt goes to stderr and a test records `process.stdout.write` to prove it. Cases 14.80–14.88. `packages/mcp-server/src/tools/register.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/main.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/lazy-session.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/src/login-prompt.ts`, `packages/mcp-server/README.md`, the runbook's *MCP server* section


- ⚙️ **Responsive and contrast measured in real viewports, not read off the source** — two suites, deliberately separate because the interesting pages sit on opposite sides of a sign-in. `apps/web/e2e-viewport/` runs the five signed-out routes across three real device viewports (390 / 768 / 1512) under its own `next dev` on a dedicated port with `reuseExistingServer: false`, so it can never silently adopt whatever is listening on 3001 and report coverage of a container it did not start. `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts` covers the signed-in shell, because the defect that motivated all this — `.gn-nav` running 10px past a 390px viewport — was on the authenticated pages and on none of the signed-out ones. **Every case asserts the viewport actually took effect by reading `window.innerWidth` back inside the page**, since this repo has a recorded failure where a resize reported success while the page stayed 1512px wide. The load-bearing case is the cross-width one: a suite passing identically at 390 and 1512 has proven nothing about responsiveness. Tap targets ratchet against a committed baseline rather than asserting a floor — the count may fall, never rise — because that reading does not move with the viewport and is recorded as an owner decision in `docs/pre-launch-checklist.md`. `apps/web/playwright.viewport.config.ts`, `apps/web/e2e-viewport/`, `apps/web/e2e-stack/viewport.spec.ts`, cases 13.254–13.255
- ⚙️ **Security headers on both apps, and the two that are deliberately absent** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — until now the product sent exactly one, `Strict-Transport-Security`. Both apps now send `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `X-Frame-Options: DENY` unconditionally; the API sends `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer` and the web app `strict-origin-when-cross-origin`, because a browser never navigates FROM an API response while a page whose links stop carrying their origin breaks flows that read it. The server's hook sits outside the `options.security` block so the ~60 test call sites that omit it can still witness the headers. **`Content-Security-Policy` and `Permissions-Policy` are deferred, and their absence is asserted** — both are statements about what a document may do, and for the web app a policy is a real design problem whose failure mode is breaking in production only. The web app also stops advertising `X-Powered-By: Next.js` — found by curling the running container, since a sweep for MISSING headers cannot notice one that is present and unwanted. Cases 13.256–13.261, 13.263. `apps/server/src/http/security-headers.ts`, `apps/web/next.config.ts`
- ⚙️ **A mobile build that would inline a working credential refuses to run** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — `EXPO_PUBLIC_DEV_TOKEN` is substituted into the bundle at build time, so a build carrying one publishes a bearer credential in plain text to whoever holds the binary. `apps/mobile/scripts/check-dev-credentials.mjs` refuses BY DEFAULT and admits it only with an explicit `GISIK_ALLOW_DEV_TOKEN=1`, because a control that needs a "this is a release" signal to be present fails open the moment that signal is absent. It scans `.env*` files as well as the environment — the way `EXPO_PUBLIC_*` is normally set, and the blind spot of an env-only guard. Wired into the `build` script with `&&`, self-testing before it certifies anything. Cases 12.390–12.393. `apps/mobile/scripts/check-dev-credentials.mjs`, `apps/mobile/package.json`
- ✅ **The phone's help screen follows the device's light/dark setting** (owner-assigned, 2026-09-08) — it resolved every colour through `colorOf('light', role)` until now, so a dark-mode phone got light colours. `useColorScheme()` selects the palette and `app.json` declares `userInterfaceStyle: automatic`, without which the OS never reports dark and the hook answers light forever. The palette lives in a sibling `.ts` with no react-native import, which is what makes both branches testable in a node runner without a simulator. Cases 12.394–12.396. `apps/mobile/src/screens/help-theme.ts`, `apps/mobile/app.json`

- ✅ **You can see and revoke the assistants connected to your account** (MCP plan Task 12, owner ruling 5) — `/mcp/tokens` lists them and revokes one in a click, replacing the plan's default of a hand-written `DELETE`. It is a PER-USER screen, not a workspace setting: an MCP token carries your permissions in every workspace you belong to, so a path with a workspace in it would say something false about what revoking does. The row shows when the connection was approved and when the assistant last used it — "Never used" when it has not, rather than a date invented to fill the column — and the page says why there is nothing else: only a one-way hash of the connection is stored. Showing last-used was an owner ruling (2026-09-08); the column was already being written on every resolution, so only the display was open. Revoking ends access at the assistant's NEXT request rather than interrupting it mid-task. `DELETE /v1/me/mcp-tokens/:id` is scoped to the caller inside the statement and answers the same 404 for "not yours" and "does not exist", so it is not an oracle for which tokens exist. Cases 14.90–14.103. `apps/server/src/routes/mcp-tokens.ts`, `apps/web/app/mcp/tokens/page.tsx`
- ⚙️ **The HTTP-Origin CSRF control covers all four mutating methods, not just POST** (found by MCP plan Task 12) — `httpOriginRefused` returned early on `method !== 'POST'` under a comment reading "mutations only". Every protected route happened to be a POST, so the control looked complete while being unable to protect a PUT, PATCH or DELETE added to the set; the forged-`Origin` DELETE reached `requireSession`, which answered 401 for the wrong reason. `security-middleware.test.ts`'s route table now carries a method column, because a table that cannot express a non-POST route cannot catch it. `apps/server/src/http/http-origin.ts`, case 14.95
- ⚙️ **The task ledger refuses a row that cites a PR which never merged** (2026-09-08) — `done` rows are validated by sha reachability, but a non-`done` row requires no evidence by design, so a wrong status carrying a stale PR number was invisible to every other check in the gate. It went wrong for real: `mcp-server/12` read `in-flight 324` while the work had landed through #329, because merging #323 deleted #324's base branch and GitHub auto-closed it. Case 13.262. `scripts/check-task-ledger.mjs`
